Tel.: +86-150-8708-7320 (X.W.); +86-136-2211-0805 (H.J.); +86-186-2941-8666 (W.W.)</p></fn><fn id="fn1-molecules-29-04454"><label>†</label><p>These authors contributed equally to this work.</p></fn></author-notes><pub-date><day>19</day><month>9</month><year>2024</year></pub-date><volume>29</volume><issue>18</issue><fpage>4454</fpage><page-range>4454</page-range><pub-history><event event-type="pmc-release"><date><day>28</day><month>9</month><year>2024</year></date></event></pub-history><permissions><copyright-statement>© 2024 by the authors.</copyright-statement><license><license-p>Licensee MDPI, Basel, Switzerland. This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" ext-link-type="uri">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</ext-link>).</license-p></license></permissions><self-uri xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454.pdf" content-type="pmc-pdf"><?cloudpmc-path 0e81/11434003/dc71a337b7e6/molecules-29-04454.pdf?><?cloudpmc-bucket app?><?size 3626929?></self-uri><abstract id="abstract1"><title>Abstract</title><p>The rapidly growing field of cannabinoid research is gaining recognition for its impact in neuropsychopharmacology and mood regulation. However, prenyltransferase (NphB) (a key enzyme in cannabinoid precursor synthesis) still needs significant improvement in order to be usable in large-scale industrial applications due to low activity and limited product range. By rational design and high-throughput screening, NphB’s catalytic efficiency and product diversity have been markedly enhanced, enabling direct production of a range of cannabinoids, without the need for traditional enzymatic conversions, thus broadening the production scope of cannabinoids, including cannabigerol (CBG), cannabigerolic acid (CBGA), cannabigerovarin (CBGV), and cannabigerovarinic acid (CBGVA). Notably, the W3 mutant achieved a 10.6-fold increase in CBG yield and exhibited a 10.3- and 20.8-fold enhancement in catalytic efficiency for CBGA and CBGV production, respectively. The W4 mutant also displayed an 9.3-fold increase in CBGVA activity. Molecular dynamics simulations revealed that strategic reconfiguration of the active site’s hydrogen bonding network, disulfide bond formation, and enhanced hydrophobic interactions are pivotal for the improved synthetic efficiency of these NphB mutants. Our findings advance the understanding of enzyme optimization for cannabinoid synthesis and lay a foundation for the industrial-scale production of these valuable compounds.</p><sec id="kwd-group1" sec-type="kwd-group" disp-level="2"><p><bold>Keywords:</bold> cannabinoids, NphB, rational design, molecular dynamics</p></sec></abstract><custom-meta-group><custom-meta><meta-name>status</meta-name><meta-value>released</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>display-pdf</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-olf</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-manuscript</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-preprint</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-journal-matter</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-scanned</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>is-retracted</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta></custom-meta-group></article-meta><notes notes-type="article-notes"><sec id="historyarticle-meta1" sec-type="history" disp-level="2"><p>Received 2024 Aug 18; Revised 2024 Sep 14; Accepted 2024 Sep 16; Collection date 2024 Sep.</p></sec></notes></front><body><sec id="sec1-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="1"><title>1. Introduction</title><p><italic>Cannabis sativa</italic> L. has been utilized for centuries, primarily due to its psychoactive constituents [<xref rid="B1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">1</xref>]. The main cannabinoids from <italic>Cannabis sativa</italic> L., including cannabigerol (CBG), cannabidiol (CBD), and cannabidiolic acid (CBDA), have shown significant potential in neuropsychopharmacology and mood regulation [<xref rid="B2-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">2</xref>,<xref rid="B3-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">3</xref>,<xref rid="B4-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">4</xref>]. Several countries have approved CBG and CBD as additives in various fields [<xref rid="B5-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">5</xref>,<xref rid="B6-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">6</xref>,<xref rid="B7-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">7</xref>,<xref rid="B8-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">8</xref>,<xref rid="B9-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">9</xref>] including food, beverages, cosmetics, and nutritional supplements [<xref rid="B10-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">10</xref>,<xref rid="B11-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">11</xref>,<xref rid="B12-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">12</xref>]. In recent years, there has been an increase in cannabinoid use in Europe and North America [<xref rid="B13-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">13</xref>,<xref rid="B14-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">14</xref>], leading to more research on <italic>Cannabis sativa</italic> L. secondary metabolites, especially cannabinoid [<xref rid="B15-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">15</xref>,<xref rid="B16-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">16</xref>,<xref rid="B17-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">17</xref>].</p><p>Traditional cannabinoid extraction methods from botanical matrices face challenges including the presence of diverse cannabinoid constituents that require complex purification protocols. Moreover, Cannabis sativa L. cultivation involves long growth cycles and extensive land use [<xref rid="B18-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">18</xref>,<xref rid="B19-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">19</xref>]. Consequently, synthetic biology has become an attractive approach to synthesize cannabinoids more efficiently [<xref rid="B20-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">20</xref>,<xref rid="B21-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="B22-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="B23-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">23</xref>]. The prenyltransferase (NphB) is the first step in combining geranyl diphosphate (GPP) and olivetolic acid (oa) to produce cannabigerolic acid (CBGA) [<xref rid="B22-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>]. NphB is capable of catalyzing the C- and O-geranylation of a variety of aromatic compounds [<xref rid="B24-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">24</xref>,<xref rid="B25-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>]. It can catalyze the geranylation of OA at the C3 position to form CBGA (minor) and at the O2 position to form 2-O-geranylolivetolic acid (2OG, major) [<xref rid="B20-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">20</xref>]. Numerous studies have reported that enhancing its product specificity and kinetic performance enables the efficient synthesis of CBGA [<xref rid="B26-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="B27-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="B28-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">28</xref>]. This is the common precursor of cannabinoid compounds in the cannabis biosynthetic pathway [<xref rid="B29-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">29</xref>,<xref rid="B30-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="B31-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">31</xref>]. CBGA could be further converted into CBDA, THCA, CBD, THC, and other cannabinoids [<xref rid="B32-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">32</xref>,<xref rid="B33-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">33</xref>,<xref rid="B34-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">34</xref>]. Although CBGA biosynthesis and some non-natural analogs have been achieved in yeast- and cell-free catalysis [<xref rid="B27-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="B35-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">35</xref>], the low catalytic activity for NphB still is a critical rate-limiting factor in the synthesis process. There is a strong need for further research into its catalytic mechanisms to improve yields.</p><p>In this study, we employed the rational design and modification of NphB to enhance the biosynthesis of various cannabinoid scaffolds. Additionally, we revealed its catalytic mechanism that results in the synthesis of different cannabinoids by NphB mutants. These results lay the foundation for synthesizing rare cannabinoid compounds.</p></sec><sec id="sec2-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="1"><title>2. Results</title><sec id="sec2dot1-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>2.1. Screening and Characterization of NphB for CBG Biosynthesis</title><p>To enhance the diversity of products, a comparative study was conducted on the structural differences of various cannabinoids. Rational design led to the selection and use of non-olivetolic acid (non-oa) substrates as alternatives to olivetolic acid (oa) for conjugation with geranyl pyrophosphate (GPP) mediated by NphB. This strategy facilitated the creation of novel cannabinoid scaffolds not derived directly from cannabigerolic acid (CBGA) but synthesized by NphB. These include cannabigerol (CBG) from olivetol (oli), cannabigerovarin (CBGV) from 5-propyl-1,3-benzenediol (ol3), and cannabigerovarinic acid (CBGVA) from 2,4-dihydroxy-6-propylbenzoic acid (oa3) (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f001" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>A). The primary objective of this study was to synthesize a spectrum of cannabinoids, with a particular focus on the biosynthesis of CBG, demonstrating the utilization of oli and GPP as precursors in NphB-catalyzed reactions.</p><fig id="molecules-29-04454-f001" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 1</label><caption><p>Screening of prenyltransferases orthologous to NphB. (<bold>A</bold>) Yield routes of various cannabinoids by NphB. (<bold>B</bold>) Gene tree for the selected homologous sequences to the previously reported NphB from Streptomyces sp. strain CL190. The GenBank accession: <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/AB187169" ext-link-type="uri">AB187169</ext-link>. (<bold>C</bold>) The CBG yield spectrum of different sequences. The error bars represent standard deviation. Measurements were conducted in triplicate. (<bold>D</bold>) A protein model for the N3 homolog, the pink region delineates the substrate-binding pocket.</p></caption><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g001.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/2ed79525e358/molecules-29-04454-g001.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 2054?><?original-width 2782?><?scaled-height 586?><?scaled-width 794?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g001.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/98ca05bb6c55/molecules-29-04454-g001.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig><p>To identify potential sequences for our synthetic biology research, the previously reported aromatic prenyltransferase Orf2 from Streptomyces sp. strain CL190 was used as the query sequence to perform a homology search in the NCBI database [<xref rid="B25-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="B30-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>]. We selected a total of 100 prenyltransferases sequences with more than 30% identity (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S1</xref>). Based on the gene tree, we delineated four discrete clades and selected 3 to 5 representative sequences from each clade for further analysis. Finally, a total of 16 prenyltransferases sequences were selected (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f001" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>B), followed by a streamlined renaming process for these selected sequences (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Table S1</xref>).</p><p>The efficiency of CBG synthesis from the various sequences was analyzed (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f001" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>C), where the sequences N3, N2, and N5 showed relatively high activities, yielding 5.8 mg/L, 5.6 mg/L, and 4.2 mg/L of CBG, respectively. The wild-type enzyme demonstrated peak activity at 30 °C with a 5 mM concentration of magnesium ions in a pH 8.0 buffered solution (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S2A–D</xref>). To boost the wild-type enzyme’s catalytic activity for CBG, we downloaded the protein structure model of the NphB sequence (1ZB6_A) from the Protein data bank (PDB) database and utilized Rosetta to dock the necessary substrates and cofactors to the active site [<xref rid="B36-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">36</xref>], followed by the optimization of the docked structure (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S3</xref>) [<xref rid="B37-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">37</xref>,<xref rid="B38-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">38</xref>,<xref rid="B39-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">39</xref>,<xref rid="B40-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">40</xref>]. Our focus was on the residues lining the substrate-binding pocket (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f001" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>D), which are critical for the enzyme’s interaction with its substrate. Conformations were deemed plausible based on two criteria: the distance between the substrate reactive site and the α-C of GPP within 5 Å and a negative docking score (binding energy). The most reasonable structure for further analysis was selected based on empirical experience. Superimposition of docking results with the Nphb-GSPP-1,6-DHN complex confirmed the reliability of our findings, illustrating that the oli occupied a similar area within the catalytic pocket, as seen with 1,6-DHN [<xref rid="B24-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">24</xref>,<xref rid="B25-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>]. The measured distance from the C5 of oli to the C1 of GPP was measured at 5.1 Å, which is comparable to the 4.2 Å observed in the complex structure of NphB with 1,6-DHN and GSPP. By combining the docking results of the N3 model with substrate interaction and kinetic analysis, we further engineered N3 to improve its catalytic efficiency in producing CBG.</p></sec><sec id="sec2dot2-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>2.2. Engineering of NphB for Cannabinoids Biosynthesis</title><p>Based on the docking results of the enzyme with the substrates, 54 residues within a 6 Å range of oli and GPP were selected for preliminary analysis as part of the substrate-binding pocket. Conservation analysis led to the exclusion of 10 conserved residues from mutation considerations (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f002" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>A). In the reaction between oli and GPP, the pyrophosphate moiety of GPP engages in hydrogen bonding with the side chains of residues K119, N173, Y175, Y216, R228, and K284, thereby securing its position (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f002" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>C). The prenyl chain of GPP is embedded in a hydrophobic environment, surrounded by hydrophobic amino acid residues. Olivetol forms hydrogen bonds with Y288 and is stabilized by π-π stacking with the phenyl ring of F213 (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f002" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>D). While residues critical for stabilizing GPP’s pyrophosphate group were excluded from the mutagenesis targets to avoid any destabilization of GPP, Y288, and F213, impacting both GPP and olivetol, are still considered viable targets for mutational analysis. To further narrow down the target sites, virtual saturation mutagenesis was performed on the residues within the substrate-binding pocket using Rosetta 2022.08. The change in binding energy for each mutant was used to assess their potential impact on enzyme activity (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f002" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>B). A decrease in binding energy indicates a more stable substrate–enzyme complex, which is favorable for catalysis. Consequently, the sites with increased binding energy among all 20 residues were eliminated. Considering the aforementioned criteria, we identified V47, V49, F107, A108, S212, F213, S214, Q161, F176, S177, T269, V271, C286, Y288, and H290 for our mutational experiments.</p><fig id="molecules-29-04454-f002" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 2</label><caption><p>Rational engineering of NphB enzyme. (<bold>A</bold>) Conservation analysis of residues within the NphB active sites. (<bold>B</bold>) Gibbs free energy heatmap of residues within the NphB active sites. The black dots represent each mutation site, and the average change in binding energy after virtual saturation mutagenesis. (<bold>C</bold>) Interaction analysis between geranyl diphosphate (GPP) and surrounding residues. (<bold>D</bold>) Interaction analysis between olivetol and surrounding residues. Yellow dashed lines represent hydrogen bonds, while green dashed lines indicate π-π stacking.</p></caption><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g002.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/25ad8b8b1cce/molecules-29-04454-g002.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 3346?><?original-width 2400?><?scaled-height 1115?><?scaled-width 800?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g002.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/ae09d2830f05/molecules-29-04454-g002.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig><p>We designed a dual-site combinatorial saturation screening for efficient screening of candidate residues. Considering that the selected mutation residues are either adjacent or very close in spatial position, this basis was used for the dual-site combinatorial saturation screening. We first used a pyrophosphate assay kit for high-throughput preliminary screenings, then cultured and purified clones showing elevated fluorescence values. We then used the purified enzymes to confirm their activity. The first round of screening identified the mutant WT-Q161R/S214H (named W1) as showing the most significant increase in CBG catalytic activity, boosting CBG production from 2.42 mg/L to 16.23 mg/L. Interestingly, the WT-S212A/F213S mutant exhibited good catalytic activity for CBGVA, possibly due to the smaller side chains of the mutated residues allowing the reaction chamber to accommodate larger substrates. Subsequent screenings continued based on W1 and WT-S212A/F213S, focusing on sites with beneficial mutations from the first round. Further mutations based on W1 led to strains W2 (W1-V49C/H290D) and W5 (W1-F176D/S177T/Y288V). Both strains achieved nearly identical CBG yields of approximately 18.62 mg/L. Further mutations based on the W2 strain led to the creation of strain W3 (W2-V47D/G286C), which marked a significant milestone in our research. This strain increased CBG yield to 25.56 mg/L (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f003" ref-type="fig">Figure 3</xref>A), a tenfold increase from the original wild-type strain, and exhibited a remarkable 10.3-fold increase in catalytic efficiency for CBGA synthesis (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f003" ref-type="fig">Figure 3</xref>B) and a 20.8-fold increase for CBGV production (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f003" ref-type="fig">Figure 3</xref>C). Additionally, the mutants W2 and W6 (W2-V47C/G286C) also showed significant improvements, with CBGV production increasing by 17.8 and 14.6 times, respectively. Furthermore, in the biosynthesis of CBGVA through the combination of oa3 and GPP, the W4 (WT-S212A/F213S-Q161R/Y288V) mutant has an 9.3-fold increase in enzyme activity compared to the wild-type (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f003" ref-type="fig">Figure 3</xref>D).</p><fig id="molecules-29-04454-f003" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 3</label><caption><p>Selection of NphB mutants for the augmented biosynthesis of diverse cannabinoids. (<bold>A</bold>) CBG production by the wild-type and mutant strains. (<bold>B</bold>) CBGA production by the wild-type and mutant strains. (<bold>C</bold>) CBGV production by the wild-type and mutant strains. (<bold>D</bold>) CBGVA production by the wild-type and mutant strains. ND signifies not detected. The error bars represent standard deviation. Measurements were conducted in triplicate. Red represents the synthesis data of CBG, blue represents the synthesis data of CBGA, green represents the synthesis data of CBGV, and yellow represents the synthesis data of CBGVA.</p></caption><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g003.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/0695983812fa/molecules-29-04454-g003.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 2046?><?original-width 2645?><?scaled-height 584?><?scaled-width 755?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g003.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/cf8db5a99fb5/molecules-29-04454-g003.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig></sec><sec id="sec2dot3-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>2.3. Mechanistic Analysis of Production of Diverse Cannabinoids</title><p>To elucidate the molecular basis for the enhanced catalysis of these mutants, we conducted a 100 ns molecular dynamics simulation for both the wild-type and the mutant, then carried out subsequent analysis after confirming the stability of the trajectory based on the root-mean-square deviation (RMSD) (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S4</xref>). we compared the wild-type NphB with the W3 variant, which showed significantly enhanced activity for four different aromatic substrates. As a promising aromatic prenyltransferase, NphB includes a cylindrical decameric β-barrel filled with solvent at its core, surrounded by α-helices exposed to the solvent. The final helix at the C-terminus forms a lid over the barrel. The W3 variant contains six-point mutations distributed within the β-barrel and at its junction with the lid (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f004" ref-type="fig">Figure 4</xref>A). The Q161R mutation has led to an increase in hydrogen bonds with surrounding water molecules from three to seven, and the number of bound water molecules has increased from three in the wild-type to five. This enhancement significantly augments the hydrophobicity within the substrate pocket, thereby stabilizing the hydrophobic segment of the substrate (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f004" ref-type="fig">Figure 4</xref>B). The E214H mutation forms a hydrogen bond with the substrate’s phenyl ring hydroxyl group, enhancing substrate stability. Additionally, a conformational change in the substrate occurs, reducing the distance between the GPP C1 and the olivetol C4 from 4.4 Å to 3.5 Å. This substantial reduction in the reactive distance between the two substrates promotes the occurrence of the reaction (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f004" ref-type="fig">Figure 4</xref>C). Mutation of residues 286 and 49 to cysteine facilitates the formation of a disulfide bond, which stabilizes the substrate-binding pocket and enhances the overall structural integrity of the enzyme. Moreover, the elevated local hydrophobicity resulting from this mutation is advantageous for the stabilization of the substrate conformation and the carbon cation intermediate (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f004" ref-type="fig">Figure 4</xref>D). Residues 47 and 290, situated at the entrance loop of the substrate-binding pocket, regulate substrate entry via conditional α-helix dynamics. In the wild-type enzyme, H290 engages in hydrogen bonding with S46 and V48, thereby limiting the mobility of the α-helix (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S5A</xref>). In the W3 mutant, the mutations H290D and V47D disrupt the pre-existing hydrogen bond network, leading to the formation of new interactions between D290 and I291 (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S5B</xref>). The reconfiguration of the hydrogen bond network diminishes the interactions of D290 with neighboring residues, alleviating the restraints imposed on the α-helix. By analyzing the root mean square fluctuation (RMSF) values of WT and mutant W3, the RMSF value of residue F176 in the WT is 0.0376 nm, while in mutant W3 it is 0.044 nm, indicating that the residue has the least fluctuation and its side-chain spatial position is relatively stable (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S6</xref>). To quantify the motion of the α-helix, we measured its distance to F176. The mutant exhibits an increased distance between the α-helix and F176, which dilates the binding pocket and enhances the α-helix flexibility, allowing for unimpeded substrate interaction (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f004" ref-type="fig">Figure 4</xref>E). This enhanced mobility facilitates greater contact between the substrate and GPP, thereby increasing the probability of the catalytic reaction.</p><fig id="molecules-29-04454-f004" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 4</label><caption><p>Mechanistic analysis of NphB mutants for diverse cannabinoid scaffolds. (<bold>A</bold>) Overall structural model of NphB, wild-type to W3 point mutations are shown as red dots. (<bold>B</bold>) The mutation of residue 161 results in a change in the quantity of water molecules interacting and the number of hydrogen bonds with these water molecules, where yellow dashed lines denote hydrogen bonds and blue spheres represent water molecules. (<bold>C</bold>) The mutation of residue 214 induces alterations in substrate conformation and a reduction in the reaction distance, yellow dashed lines denote hydrogen bonds, and purple dashed lines signify the reaction distances. (<bold>D</bold>) Residues 286 and 49 are mutated to cysteine, resulting in the formation of a disulfide bond. (<bold>E</bold>) The mutations at positions 47 and 290 increase the flexibility of the α-helix and expand the substrate pocket, where the gray structure denotes the wild-type enzyme, and the purple structure corresponds to the W3 mutant variant. All gray spheres represent metal ions.</p></caption><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g004.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/8c13f0a98aa4/molecules-29-04454-g004.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 2564?><?original-width 2989?><?scaled-height 641?><?scaled-width 747?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g004.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/c4e872169db2/molecules-29-04454-g004.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig><p>During the screening process, a special mutant W4 was identified, which only had four mutations (S212A, F213S, Q161R and Y288V). Compared to the wild-type, its activity for CBG and CBGV has almost vanished. However, the synthetic efficiency for CBGA and CBGVA has significantly increased, with CBGVA showing the highest activity. W4 exhibits superior catalytic efficiency for substrates with carboxylic groups. Structural analysis reveals that residues S212A, F213S, and Y288V are located near the aromatic ring of the substrate. These mutations introduce smaller side chains compared to the wild-type, creating additional space within the β-barrel. The substrate-binding pocket volume expanded from 1549.5 Å<sup>3</sup> in the wild-type to 2209.4 Å<sup>3</sup> in the W4 variant (<xref rid="molecules-29-04454-f005" ref-type="fig">Figure 5</xref>A,B). This expanded binding pocket better accommodates substrates with larger carboxyl groups, enhancing the forward reaction efficiency.</p><fig id="molecules-29-04454-f005" position="float"><?disp-level 3?><label>Figure 5</label><caption><p>Structural and substrate-binding pocket size comparison between the wild-type (<bold>A</bold>) and the W4 mutant (<bold>B</bold>) variant.</p></caption><alternatives><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="image" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g005.jpg"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/d060afedf4c8/molecules-29-04454-g005.jpg?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?><?image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?original-height 1280?><?original-width 2876?><?scaled-height 320?><?scaled-width 719?></graphic><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="thumb" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-g005.gif"><?cloudpmc-path blobs/0e81/11434003/7ad14e34b596/molecules-29-04454-g005.gif?><?cloudpmc-bucket cdn?></graphic></alternatives></fig></sec></sec><sec id="sec3-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="1"><title>3. Discussion</title><p>Using computational simulations, we refined NphB to accommodate various substrates, substantially increasing yields of different cannabinoids through one-step catalysis by NphB. Previous studies have focused on enhancing the product specificity and kinetic performance of NphB to increase the yield of CBGA [<xref rid="B21-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="B41-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">41</xref>]. Mutants such as Y288A_G286S or Y288V_A232S dramatically improved NphB kinetics; such point mutations also rendered almost exclusive production of CBGA, leading to 21-fold increase in CBGA titer (744 mg/L) [<xref rid="B26-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="B28-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">28</xref>]. Using a continuous reaction setup in a cell-free system also increased the CBGA titer to 1.25 g/L [<xref rid="B27-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>]. Furthermore, the product specificity for CBGA was enhanced by the Q295F mutation, which increased the in vivo activity in E. coli by 20-fold [<xref rid="B42-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">42</xref>]. Building on this work, we conducted detailed mutagenesis and screening of critical NphB enzyme sites. We identified several key amino acid residues that are pivotal for the enzyme’s activity and specificity. These residues include Q161, S214, H290, G286, S212, and F213. By introducing mutations at these sites, either individually or in various combinations, we have achieved differentiated enhancements in the production of several cannabinoids, including CBG, CBGA, CBGVA, and CBGV. Each mutation or combination of mutations was strategically chosen based on their potential to alter the enzyme’s spatial configuration or its dynamic interaction with specific substrates. The analysis of the catalytic mechanisms of different NphB mutants lays a solid foundation for future enhancements of the NphB enzyme. Our results provide novel insights into enzyme catalytic mechanisms and lay the groundwork for identifying even higher-activity variants in the future. Based on the reaction mechanism, mutations that stabilize substrate orientation, stabilize the intermediate, and promote substrate entry and exit can influence the enzyme’s activity from different perspectives.</p><p>Cannabinoids continue to be investigated for their prospective therapeutic utilities, with over 113 distinct scaffolds identified thus far [<xref rid="B43-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">43</xref>]. While CBG has attracted substantial attention, other cannabinoids also demonstrate promising medicinal attributes. As additional cannabinoids emerge, innovative bioproduction platforms become imperative. Traditionally, the biosynthetic pathway for cannabinoids involves the catalysis of olivetol and GPP by the enzyme NphB to produce CBGA. Subsequently, using CBGA as a substrate, other cannabinoids such as THCA, CBDA, and CBCA can be synthesized under the catalysis of other enzymes. We developed an innovative approach leveraging NphB’s capabilities in response to the growing diversity of cannabinoids and their potential applications. We have engineered NphB to perform a one-step catalysis that directly produces various cannabinoids, bypassing the need for subsequent enzymatic transformations typically required in traditional pathways. This streamlined approach not only significantly enhances the efficiency of cannabinoid synthesis but also improves carbon utilization within the biosynthetic pathways.</p><p>By reducing the enzymatic steps involved, we minimize the metabolic burden on the host systems and enhance the overall yield and purity of the desired cannabinoids. Our approach has fundamentally enhanced the efficiency of cannabinoid synthesis and improved carbon utilization within the biosynthetic pathways. There is potential to transplant these modified NphB enzymes into living systems or incorporate them into multi-enzyme, cell-free configurations for the industrial production of cannabinoids from readily available precursors.</p></sec><sec id="sec4-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="1"><title>4. Materials and Methods</title><sec id="sec4dot1-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.1. Genes and Reagents</title><p>Genes were synthesized by Wuhan Genecreat Biological Engineering Co., Ltd. (Wuhan, China) and primer synthesis was completed by Anshengda Biotechnology Co., Ltd. (Tianjing, China). Mevalonate (MVA), cannabigerol (CBG), and olivetolic acid (oa) were sourced from Sigma-Aldrich (Shanghai, China). Other cannabinoid substrates and analytical standards were provided by Macklin (Shanghai, China). BL21(DE3) chemically competent <italic>E. coli</italic> was purchased from Beijing TransGen Biotech Co., Ltd. (Beijing, China).</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot2-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.2. Evolution of NphB Enzyme Family</title><p>To identify homologous sequences, we used the aromatic prenyltransferase Orf2 from Streptomyces sp. strain CL190 as a query template in the National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI) Protein Data Bank, retrieving 100 sequences. Multiple sequence alignment was performed with ClustalW, followed by Gblock extraction of conserved regions (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S7</xref>). Phylogenetic analysis was then conducted in MEGA to construct a gene tree, enabling selection of 16 enzyme candidates for subsequent experimentation.</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot3-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.3. Cloning, Expression and Purification of Enzymes</title><p>The genes were synthesized by Wuhan Genecreate Bioengineering Co., Ltd. (Wuhan, China) and subcloned into the pET-28a(+) vector via one-step clone assembly. All plasmids were transformed into <italic>E. coli</italic> BL21(DE3), and a single colony was inoculated into 5 mL LB (with 100 μg/mL kanamycin) and grown overnight (220 rpm, 37 °C). This starter culture was diluted into 800 mL 2xYT (with 100 μg/mL kanamycin) and incubated (220 rpm, 37 °C) until an OD<sub>600</sub> 0.6–0.8 was attained. Protein expression was then induced by adding isopropyl β-D-1-thiogalactopyranoside (IPTG) to a final concentration of 0.5 mM, followed by incubation at 16 °C for 16–20 h. The induced culture was centrifuged (5500 rpm, 10 min, 4 °C), the supernatant discarded, and the cell pellet resuspended in lysis buffer (50 mM Tris, 150 mM NaCl, pH 8.0). The suspension underwent high-pressure homogenization (800–1000 bar, 4 °C, 3–5 min) and was clarified by centrifugation (8000 rpm, 1 h, 4 °C). The resulting supernatant was subjected to immobilized nickel affinity chromatography, washing with buffer (50 mM Tris pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 20 mM imidazole) to remove impurities, and eluting the target protein with buffer (50 mM Tris pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl, 200 mM imidazole). The eluate was concentrated and stored at −80 °C.</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot4-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.4. Catalytic Conditions for CBG Synthesis by NphB Homologous Sequences</title><p>To evaluate the catalytic proficiency of NphB homologs, reactions were initiated by combining enzymes and cofactors in 50 μL volumes, with final concentrations of 0.5 mM GPP, 1 mM olivetol, 5 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, and 50 mM Tris (pH 8.0), and 1.0 mg/mL NphB. Following 8 h incubation at 30 °C, reactions were terminated via addition of 100 μL ethyl acetate, shaking (5–10 s), and centrifuged (12,000× <italic>g</italic>, 3 min) to recover the organic fraction in fresh tubes. The aqueous fraction underwent identical ethyl acetate extraction and pooling of the combined organic layers, drying under vacuum centrifugation, and reconstituting in 50 μL methanol for high performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) analysis. By employing the method of controlled variables, the optimal metal ions, optimal metal ion concentration, optimal temperature, and optimal pH for NphB were tested.</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot5-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.5. Modification of Prenyltransferase NphB</title><p>To probe olivetol binding and its impacts on NphB activity, in silico molecular docking studies were undertaken utilizing Rosetta Ligand Dock. The crystal structure of NphB complexed with substrate (PDB ID: 1ZB6) was obtained from the RCSB Protein Data Bank. The native ligands GSPP and 1,6-dihydroxynaphtalene were removed, leaving the position of the metal ions intact. Using Rosetta, GPP and olivetol were docked into the catalytic center. Rosetta relax was then used to generate 200 models and the lowest energy structure was selected for further docking to optimize the complex (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S8</xref>). The olivetol substrate structure was downloaded from Zinc and prepared via Schrödinger conformational searches, furnishing a sdf file. The Rosetta molfile_to_params script enabled sdf to params file format conversion alongside the generation of a multi-constant image file. Following ligand and receptor processing, docking was executed with the Rosetta (rosetta_scripts.mpi.linuxgccrelease) application utilizing StartFrom and Transform protocols and 10,000 models. Resulting complexes were ranked by total score and binding energy, with the top 10% visually inspected to eliminate unreasonable poses. The lowest energy binding mode was selected as the final docked complex. Subsequent point mutations employed Rosetta Cartesian_ddG calculations, initially performing two rounds of relax on the complex (200 models each) and utilizing the lowest energy structure for further refinement of all residues within 5 Å of the substrate. The refined complex then underwent saturation mutagenesis of these proximal residues to all other 19 amino acids, evaluating the change in binding energy. Finally, mutants exhibiting both favorable binding energy changes and large magnitude effects were experimentally validated.</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot6-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.6. Screening of Prenyltransferase Saturated Mutant Library</title><p>To screen the initial NphB mutant library, we designed double-site combinatorial saturation mutagenesis primers based on the selected mutation target sites, considering that the chosen mutated residues are adjacent or in close spatial proximity. Variants were constructed using these primers and then transformed into BL21(DE3). Single colonies were inoculated into 96-well plates containing 1 mL LB medium per well supplemented with 100 μg/mL kanamycin and grown overnight (37 °C, 750 rpm). The next day, cultures were diluted to 1% into fresh 96-well plates (1 mL 2YT medium plus 100 μg/mL kanamycin per well) and incubated (37 °C, 750 rpm) until an OD<sub>600</sub> 0.4–0.6. Protein expression was induced by adding 0.5 mM IPTG followed by 14–16 h at 16 °C. After centrifugation (3500 rpm, 5 min) to harvest cells, pellets were washed in buffer (50 mM Tris pH 8.0, 150 mM NaCl), lysed, and 30 μL magnetic beads were added per well to immobilize proteins. Beads were washed in buffer before adding the reaction components (0.5 mM olivetol, 0.25 mM geranyl pyrophosphate (GPP), 5 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 50 mM Tris pH 8.0, 40 μL final volume) and incubating at 30 °C for 2 h. Preliminary screening relied on a pyrophosphate fluorescence kit, measuring the catalytic activity of the mutants in the initial screening by detecting the fluorescence value of the residual pyrophosphate groups after the reaction. If no mutants with fluorescence values higher than the wild-type were detected in the initial screening, it was considered that no positive mutations occurred at that site. Variants with increased fluorescence were selected as the focus mutation library for scale-up cultivation. The 800 mL expression and purification of NphB variants were performed as previously described. The enzymatic assay consisted of 0.25 mM GPP, 0.5 mM olivetol, 5 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 50 mM Tris pH 8.0, and 3 mg/mL NphB in a total volume of 40 μL, incubated at 30 °C for 2 h. The reaction was quenched with ethyl acetate, reconstituted in 50 μL methanol, and analyzed by HPLC as previously detailed.</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot7-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.7. In Vitro Synthesis of CBG, CBGA, CBGV and CBGVA</title><p>To evaluate the catalytic proficiency of NphB mutants, reactions were initiated by combining enzymes and cofactors in 50 μL volumes, with final concentrations of 0.25 mM GPP, 0.5 mM olivetol (oa/ol3/oa3), 5 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, and 50 mM Tris (pH 8.0), and 1.0 mg/mL NphB. Following 6 h incubation at 30 °C, reactions were terminated via addition of 100 μL ethyl acetate, shaking (5–10 s), and centrifuged (12,000× <italic>g</italic>, 3 min) to recover the organic fraction in fresh tubes. The aqueous fraction underwent identical ethyl acetate extraction and pooling of the combined organic layers, drying under vacuum centrifugation, and reconstituting in 50 μL methanol for high performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) analysis (<xref rid="app1-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="sec">Figure S9</xref>). CBG quantitation relied on reverse phase fractionation over a C18 column (250 mm × 4.6 mm × 5 μm) using an Alliance Waters e2695 HPLC system with 1 mL/min flow rate. Gradient elution employed water + 0.1% formic acid (solvent A) and acetonitrile + 0.1% formic acid (solvent B) mobile phases, holding B at 70% for 1 min followed by a 6 min increase to 77%, holding for 10 min, and re-equilibration to 70% B, totaling 26 min.</p></sec><sec id="sec4dot8-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="2"><title>4.8. Molecular Dynamics</title><p>Molecular dynamics simulations were performed using the GRMOMACS 2020.6 package, the protein system used Amber14sb force field, the general amber force field (GAFF) was used for the ligands [<xref rid="B44-molecules-29-04454" ref-type="bibr">44</xref>]. The TIP3P water model with an edge of 10 Å was used to solvate the complex system in a cubic box. The charge was neutralized by adding Sodium ions. The long-range electrostatic interactions were treated using the particle mesh Ewald (PME) method with a 1.0 nm cutoff. Minimization was performed by 5000 steps of steepest-descent minimization procedure. Subsequently, the NVT (constant number of atoms, volume, and temperature) and NPT (constant number of atoms, pressure, and temperature) ensemble were used to carry out a 100 ps restricted simulation. Finally, the maximum cluster of structures in the trajectory between 40 and 50 ns of simulations was used for structure analysis. PyMOL was used for structural visualization and structural analysis.</p></sec></sec><sec id="sec5-molecules-29-04454" disp-level="1"><title>5. Conclusions</title><p>NphB is often used as a replacement in the prenylation step for producing cannabinoid cannabigerolic acid (CBGA). Our research highlights the untapped potential of NphB in the synthesis of diverse cannabinoids, with a focus on the rarer variants. Through rational design strategies, we have optimized the microenvironment within the NphB substrate-binding pocket, significantly improving the synthesis efficiency of CBG, CBGA, CBGV, and CBGVA. Molecular dynamics simulations have revealed that key factors in enhancing the synthetic capabilities of NphB mutants include the reconfiguration of the active site’s hydrogen bonding network, disulfide bond formation, and augmented hydrophobic interactions. These insights offer valuable guidance for the future refinement of the NphB enzyme and contribute to the broader understanding of protein engineering efforts catalyzed by prenyltransferases.</p></sec><sec id="ack1" sec-type="ack" disp-level="1"><title>Acknowledgments</title><p>This work has received support from Jiaxing Synbiolab Technology Co., Ltd. We would like to thank all the staff in Jiaxing Synbiolab Technology Co., Ltd. for their support and help in this project.</p></sec><sec id="app1-molecules-29-04454" sec-type="app" disp-level="1"><title>Supplementary Materials</title><p>The following supporting information can be downloaded at: <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.mdpi.com/article/10.3390/molecules29184454/s1" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.mdpi.com/article/10.3390/molecules29184454/s1</ext-link>, Table S1: Streamlined nomenclature for the 16 selected prenyltransferase sequences. Figure S1: The construction of a gene tree based on the homologous sequences of NphB. Figure S2: Investigating the reaction conditions influencing NphB’s efficiency in CBG synthesis. Figure S3: Protein NphB model construction and substrate docking. Figure S4: The RMSD values of the wild-type (A), mutant W3 (B) and mutant W4 (C). Figure S5: Analysis of the hydrogen bond network at positions 47 and 290 in both the wild-type and W3 mutant strains. Figure S6: The RMSF values of the wild-type (A) and mutant W3 (B). Figure S7: Alignment of amino acid sequences of NphB with its homologous sequences. Figure S8. The predicted binding free energies by molecular docking oa/oa3/oli/ol3 into WT-NphB. Figure S9. High-performance liquid phase detection of CBG, CBGA, CBGV and CBGVA.</p><supplementary-material id="molecules-29-04454-s001" position="float"><media xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="molecules-29-04454-s001.zip" mimetype="application" mime-subtype="zip"><?cloudpmc-path 0e81/11434003/ea6a0abd76d0/molecules-29-04454-s001.zip?><?cloudpmc-bucket app?><?size 2616494?></media></supplementary-material></sec><sec id="notes1" disp-level="1"><title>Author Contributions</title><p>Conceptualization, W.W., H.J. and X.W.; methodology, S.L., H.C. and W.X.; software, S.L., H.C., W.X. and X.J.; validation, W.X., L.H., T.B. and X.C.; formal analysis, S.L. and W.X.; resources, W.W., H.J. and X.W.; data curation, S.L. and W.X.; writing—original draft preparation, W.X., S.L. and X.C.; writing—review and editing, W.X., W.W., H.J. and X.W.; visualization, S.L. and W.X.; supervision, W.W., H.J. and X.W.; project administration, W.X.; funding acquisition, W.W. and X.W. All authors have read and agreed to the published version of the manuscript.</p></sec><sec id="notes2" disp-level="1"><title>Institutional Review Board Statement</title><p>Not applicable.</p></sec><sec id="notes3" disp-level="1"><title>Informed Consent Statement</title><p>Not applicable.</p></sec><sec id="notes4" disp-level="1"><title>Data Availability Statement</title><p>The raw data that support this study are available from the corresponding authors upon reasonable request.</p></sec><sec id="notes5" disp-level="1"><title>Conflicts of Interest</title><p>Jiaxing Synbiolab Technology Co., Ltd. has filed a Chinese patent application for the NphB-catalyzed synthesis of CBG or CBGA. To view a copy of this licence, visit <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" ext-link-type="uri" xlink:href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/">http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/</ext-link>.</license-p></license></permissions><self-uri xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="pmc-pdf" xlink:href="42003_2025_Article_7509.pdf"><?pdf-name 42003_2025_Article_7509.pdf?><?pdf-size 1365061?><?pdf-md5 737a000ea299f479fc48ce60d5d26ff4?><?pdf-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?pdf-cloudpmc-urn urn:app:0f1f/11794439/737a000ea299/42003_2025_Article_7509.pdf?></self-uri><abstract id="Abs1"><p id="Par1">Cannabinoids are unique meroterpenoids, with cannabigerolic acid (CBGA) serving as a dedicated precursor. This study introduces a fungal aromatic prenyltransferase AscC into the engineered <italic toggle="yes">Escherichia coli</italic> to catalyze the transfer of C<sub>5</sub>-C<sub>15</sub> terpenoid linear precursors to olivetolic acid. Four CBGA derivatives (compounds <bold>1–4</bold>) with diverse C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, or C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chains are isolated and identified, with compound <bold>4</bold> being an undescribed product featuring a C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chain at the C-5 position. Compound <bold>4</bold> demonstrates the highest anti-neuroinflammatory and antibacterial activities, with IC<sub>50</sub> values of 3.06 µM for TNF-α and 4.31 µM for IL-6, alongside EC<sub>50</sub> values ranging from 0.87 to 3.16 µM against three Gram-positive bacteria. An efficient construct is established by incorporating an additional copy of AscC, resulting in a yield of 14.85 ± 0.91 mg/L of compound <bold>4</bold>. Two mutants, L180Y and L180F, are engineered to selectively produce compound <bold>4</bold>. These findings provide a foundation for enriching the chemical diversity of bioactive cannabinoid analogs with various prenyl moieties through combinatorial biosynthesis.</p><p id="Par2"> <graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="d33e246" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 46447?><?image-md5 87c6217a421b7c4bb939bc9c03e01fb2?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 467?><?image-original-width 950?><?image-scaled-height 311?><?image-scaled-width 633?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/87c6217a421b/42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 5423?><?thumb-md5 8294375f765c9425c4f449aa4790a9fd?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 80?><?thumb-scaled-width 162?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/8294375f765c/42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.gif?></graphic> </p></abstract><abstract id="Abs2" abstract-type="web-summary"><p id="Par3">Four cannabinoid derivatives are biosynthesized in engineered bacteria. Two mutants are engineered to selectively produce an undescribed compound with a C15 prenyl chain at the C-5 position and antineuroinflammatory and antibacterial activities.</p></abstract><kwd-group kwd-group-type="npg-subject"><title>Subject terms</title><kwd>Enzyme mechanisms</kwd><kwd>Biocatalysis</kwd></kwd-group><funding-group><award-group><funding-source><institution-wrap><institution-id BY-NC-ND</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>issue-copyright-statement</meta-name><meta-value>© Springer Nature Limited 2025</meta-value></custom-meta></custom-meta-group></article-meta></front><body><sec id="Sec1" sec-type="introduction"><title>Introduction</title><p id="Par4">Natural products play pivotal roles in chemical biology and drug discovery due to their unique chemical structures and specific biological targets<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR1">1</xref></sup>. The construction of natural product analogs is important for exploring structure-activity relationships, developing drugs with enhanced bioactivity and bioavailability, and uncovering disease mechanisms as chemical biology tools<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR2">2</xref></sup>. With the advances in synthetic biology, combinatorial biosynthesis exploiting enzymatic and pathway promiscuity provides an alternative approach to generate novel natural product analogs<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR2">2</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR5">5</xref></sup>.</p><p id="Par5">Meroterpenoids represent a class of natural products with conspicuous biological activities derived from the terpenoid biosynthetic pathway coupled with other biogenic pathways. Among these, cannabinoids stand out as unique meroterpenoids initially isolated from the plant <italic toggle="yes">Cannabis sativa</italic>, a herbal medicine with a history spanning over 3500 years<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR6">6</xref></sup>. More than 120 phytocannabinoids have been isolated from <italic toggle="yes">Cannabis</italic>, most of which are alkyl resorcinol derivatives with a monoterpenoid unit<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR7">7</xref></sup>. Notably, cannabinoids, such as Δ<sup>9</sup>-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) and cannabidiol (CBD), exhibit potent pharmacological effects, including analgesic, antiemetic, anti-spasmodic, antimicrobial, and anti-inflammatory activities<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR8">8</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR10">10</xref></sup>, which renders them of great medicinal importance<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR11">11</xref></sup>. The pharmacological activities of cannabinoids principally occur through interacting with cannabinoid receptors CB1 and CB2, belonging to the superfamily of G-protein coupled receptors which mediate most of physiological responses and so have great potential as therapeutic targets for a broad spectrum of diseases<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR12">12</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR13">13</xref></sup>. The important influence of the C-3 side chain of cannabinoids on the pharmacological activities has been identified through chemical synthesis of a series of C-3 alkyl analogs<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR14">14</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR16">16</xref></sup>, however, the effect of the prenyl chain on pharmaceutical properties remains unexplored.</p><p id="Par6">The biosynthesis of cannabinoids is derived from a monoterpenoid unit produced by 2-<italic toggle="yes">C</italic>-methyl-D-erythritol 4-phosphate (MEP) pathway and an aromatic moiety olivetolic acid (OA) catalyzed by an olivetol synthase and an OA cyclase using malonyl-CoA and hexanoyl-CoA as substrates<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR17">17</xref></sup>. Cannabigerolic acid (CBGA) is the committed biosynthetic precursor of cannabinoids, formed by a C-C Friedel-Craft alkylation of OA with geranyl diphosphate (C<sub>10</sub>, GDP)<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR18">18</xref></sup>. Subsequently, the geranyl moiety of CBGA undergoes cyclization reaction to form cannabinoids such as Δ<sup>9</sup>-tetrahydrocannabinolic acid (THCA), cannabidiolic acid (CBDA), or cannabichromenic acid (CBCA), which can be decarboxylated to their neutral forms (Fig. <xref rid="Fig1" ref-type="fig">1</xref>)<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR19">19</xref></sup>.<fig id="Fig1" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 1</label><caption><p>The biosynthetic pathway of cannabinoids.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e348" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 76817?><?image-md5 eebf2bdab2c685ba9ed413d6cb2d1e30?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1463?><?image-original-width 1749?><?image-scaled-height 585?><?image-scaled-width 699?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/eebf2bdab2c6/42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 2956?><?thumb-md5 6f7d19eca7bba26f14b9d37584c1bafd?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 84?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/6f7d19eca7bb/42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p><p id="Par7">Heterologous biosynthesis of cannabinoids has been achieved in engineered yeasts, paving an alternative way for access to cannabinoids<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR22">22</xref></sup>. Based on enzyme promiscuity and engineered biosynthetic pathways, a series of unnatural cannabinoid analogs with distinct alkyl moieties at the C-3 position were obtained by combinatorial biosynthesis through feeding different fatty acids<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR23">23</xref></sup>. Given the diversity of terpenoids which includes hemiterpenoids, monoterpenoids, sesquiterpenoids, diterpenoids, and sesterterpenoids, we are particularly interested in the biosynthesis of CBGA analogs with distinct terpenoid chains. Several aromatic prenyltransferases have been reported, among which AscC, a UbiA-type prenyltransferase from <italic toggle="yes">Fusarium</italic> sp. NBRC 100844, could catalyze the conversion of 2-methyl-4,6-dihydroxy benzaldehyde into the corresponding farnesylated product<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR24">24</xref></sup>. In this work, we synthesized four CBGA analogs <bold>1–4</bold> with a C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, or C<sub>15</sub> prenyl unit using the engineered <italic toggle="yes">Escherichia coli</italic> harboring the AscC gene. Molecular docking and site-directed mutagenesis resulted in two mutants selectively producing the undescribed compoud <bold>4</bold>. In addition, the anti-neuroinflammatory and antibacterial activities of these compounds were observed. </p></sec><sec id="Sec2" sec-type="results"><title>Results</title><sec id="Sec3"><title>Heterologous expression of promiscuous aromatic prenyltransferases in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains for cannabinoid analogs production</title><p id="Par8">To biosynthesize cannabinoid analogs featuring diverse prenyl side chains, the engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains were recruited, which contain an exogenous MVA pathway along with a farnesyl diphosphate synthase (FDPS) or geranylgeranyl diphosphate (GGDPS) or geranylfarnesyl diphosphate synthase (GFDPS) previously constructed for terpenoid biosynthesis<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR25">25</xref></sup>. The aromatic prenyltransferase (CsPT4) from <italic toggle="yes">C. sativa</italic> and four fungal promiscuous aromatic prenyltransferases (aPTases), including AscC from <italic toggle="yes">Fusarium</italic> sp., Trt2 and AtaPT from <italic toggle="yes">Aspergillus terreus</italic>, and Orf2 from <italic toggle="yes">Streptomyces</italic> sp.<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR24">24</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR26">26</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR29">29</xref></sup>. were picked out to test their activity for transferring various prenyl groups to the aromatic ring of OA (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">1</xref>). The full-length cDNA of these aPTases was synthesized after codon optimization and then constructed into the corresponding expression vector.</p><p id="Par9">The four fungal aromatic prenyltransferases could express substantial amounts of recombinant proteins with expected molecular mass in the soluble fraction. These aPTases were subsequently transformed into the engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains producing C<sub>5</sub>-C<sub>25</sub> prenyl diphosphates. By feeding with OA, four specific peaks could be detected in the extract of engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> containing AscC using high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) and liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS) analysis (Fig. <xref rid="Fig2" ref-type="fig">2</xref>). The pseudo-molecular ion peaks ([M-H]<sup>–</sup>) of specific products were 291.1601, 359.2226, 427.2853, and 427.2851, indicating that AscC could catalyze the introduction of C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, and C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chains into OA (Fig. <xref rid="Fig2" ref-type="fig">2</xref>). The conversion rate of OA was 70.48% by AscC. By contrast, no obvious product was observed in the engineered bacteria harboring Trt2, AtaPT, and Orf2. In addition, we tested the catalytic activity of AscC towards a series of OA analogs (<bold>S1</bold>-<bold>S7</bold>), but no products were observed (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">2</xref>). Compared to OA, these analogs lack a hydroxyl group at the C-4 position, except <bold>S4</bold> that has an ester group at the C-1 position instead of a carboxyl group. These results suggest that both the C-4 hydroxyl group and the C-1 carboxyl group in the substrate are important for enzyme activity of AscC.<fig id="Fig2" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 2</label><caption><title>The conversion of olivetolic acid (OA) to prenylation products by engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC.</title><p><bold>A</bold> HPLC analysis of the extract of engineered bacteria harboring pET28a/AscC (a) and empty vector pET28a (b). <bold>B</bold> The mass spectrometry of peaks 1–4.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e488" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 72821?><?image-md5 f0bee20117c83f243026ffbd2ed347c3?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1048?><?image-original-width 1349?><?image-scaled-height 524?><?image-scaled-width 674?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/f0bee20117c8/42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 2943?><?thumb-md5 7cc1786ba170d159b4e3f726e87027fc?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 79?><?thumb-scaled-width 102?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/7cc1786ba170/42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p></sec><sec id="Sec4"><title>Identification of cannabinoid analogs produced by AscC in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></title><p id="Par10">The plasmid pET28a/AscC was transferred into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strain BL21 harboring the pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS plasmid, which contains a complete gene set of the mevalonate (MVA) pathway and a FDPS gene<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR30">30</xref></sup>, resulting in an engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strain BPA1. To elucidate the structure of cannabinoid analogs, a 10 L scale fermentation of the engineered BPA1 strain was performed and extracted with ethyl acetate. The <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> extracts were further purified with a combination of repeated silica-gel and Sephadex LH-20 column chromatography. Afterward, semi-preparative HPLC was carried out using a mixture of acetonitrile and acid water as a mobile phase to afford compounds <bold>1–4</bold>. Notably, compound <bold>4</bold> emerged as the predominant component (Fig. <xref rid="Fig2" ref-type="fig">2</xref>).</p><p id="Par11">Compound <bold>4</bold> was obtained as a yellowish powder and characterized by a molecular formula of C<sub>27</sub>H<sub>40</sub>O<sub>4</sub> with eight degrees of unsaturation based on high-resolution electron ionization mass spectroscopy (HR-EI-MS) (<italic toggle="yes">m/z</italic> 427.2851 [M-H]<sup>-</sup>, calcd. 427.2854) and NMR spectroscopic data (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">3</xref>–<xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">10</xref>). Compound <bold>4</bold> showed the same molecular weight as compound <bold>3</bold> but a different retention time, implying that they might be prenylated products of OA at different positions. Compound <bold>3</bold> was determined as a cannabinoid analog 2,4-dihydroxy-3-[(2<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>,6<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>)-3,7,11-trimethyl-2,6,10-dodecatrien-1-yl]-6-pentylbenzoic acid through careful inspection of the NMR data (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">11</xref>, <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">12</xref>) and comparison with those reported in the literature<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR31">31</xref></sup>. The 1D and 2D NMR spectra of <bold>4</bold> closely resembled those of <bold>3</bold> with a key difference being the upfield movement of the carbon at C-3 (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 102.2) and C-1′′ (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 31.5) in <bold>4</bold> compared to <bold>3</bold> (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 111.1 for C-3 and <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 36.7 for C-1′′). Additionally, the carbon at C-5 (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 119.9) and C-1′ (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 25.1) in <bold>4</bold> moved downfield compared to <bold>3</bold> (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 111.6 for C-5 and <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 22.2 for C-1′) indicating a C<sub>15</sub> farnesyl chain attached to C-5. Confirmation of this attachment was provided by the <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H correlation spectroscopy (COSY) correlations of H-1′/H-2′, H-4′/H-5′/H-6′ as well as heteronuclear multiple bond correlation (HMBC) correlations of H-1′ with C-4/C-5/C-6/C-2′/C-3′ (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). The <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H COSY correlations of H-1′′/H-2′′/H-3′′/H-4′′/H-5′′, combined with the HMBC correlations of H-1′′ with C-1/C-5/C-6, provided evidence for the attachment of the <italic toggle="yes">n</italic>-pentyl chain to C-6 and confirmed the planar structure of <bold>4</bold> (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). Accordingly, the structure of <bold>4</bold> was determined as 2,4-dihydroxy-5-[(2<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>,6<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>)-3,7,11-trimethyl-2,6,10-dodecatrien-1-yl]-6-pentylbenzoic acid (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). The structures of compounds <bold>1</bold> and <bold>2</bold> were identified as 2,4-dihydroxy-3-(3-methylbut-2-en-1-yl)-6-pentylbenzoic acid<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR32">32</xref></sup> and CBGA<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR33">33</xref></sup> (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">13</xref>–<xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">16</xref>), respectively, through comparison of their NMR data with those reported in the literature (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). Compounds <bold>1–4</bold> are cannabinoid analogs, in which compound <bold>4</bold> is an undescribed compound. The results indicated that engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC could produce four cannabinoid analogs with a C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, or C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chain, likely attributed to the spacious hydrophobic substrate-binding pocket of promiscuous aPTases<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR34">34</xref></sup>.<fig id="Fig3" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 3</label><caption><p>Chemical structures of the products produced by engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC and key <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H COSY and HMBC correlations of compound <bold>4</bold>.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e719" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 46529?><?image-md5 05d6f657d194190abae46921a77896e5?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 775?><?image-original-width 2000?><?image-scaled-height 310?><?image-scaled-width 800?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/05d6f657d194/42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 3408?><?thumb-md5 c6f5520d394ebf732b42cef5eb15f8c0?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 78?><?thumb-scaled-width 200?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/c6f5520d394e/42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p></sec><sec id="Sec5"><title>Compounds 1–4 displayed potent anti-inflammatory and antibacterial activities</title><p id="Par12">Cannabinoids are known for their potent anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive properties, likely through interacting with G protein-coupled receptors that are closely related to immune regulation and neurodegeneration<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR12">12</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR13">13</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR35">35</xref></sup>. Therefore, four cannabinoid analogs were evaluated for their anti-neuroinflammatory activity by investigating the inhibition of TNF-α and IL-6 secretion in BV-2 microglial cells. As shown in Table <xref rid="Tab1" ref-type="table">1</xref>, compound <bold>4</bold> showed potent inhibitory effects on TNF-α and IL-6 secretion, with IC<sub>50</sub> values of 3.06 and 4.31 µM, respectively. Compounds <bold>1–3</bold> showed moderate activity against TNF-α and IL-6 secretion with 34.29–48.17% inhibition rates at 20 µM. By comparing the structures and activity of compounds <bold>1–4</bold>, it can be concluded that the C<sub>15</sub> farnesyl unit at the C-5 position of OA played an important role in anti-inflammatory activity.<table-wrap id="Tab1" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Table 1</label><caption><p>Inhibitory effects of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> on TNF-α and IL-6 secretion of BV-2 microglial cells induced by LPS</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><thead><tr><th rowspan="2" colspan="1">Compound</th><th colspan="3" rowspan="1">TNF-α secretion</th><th colspan="2" rowspan="1">IL-6 secretion</th></tr><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibition rate (%)<sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></th><th colspan="2" rowspan="1">IC<sub>50</sub> (µM)</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibition rate (%)<sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">IC<sub>50</sub> (µM)</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>1</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">34.29 ± 6.84</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">35.01 ± 5.94</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>2</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">40.62 ± 7.93</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">48.17 ± 5.57</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>3</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">41.20 ± 6.36</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">46.21 ± 5.84</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>4</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">85.35 ± 7.32</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">3.06</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">81.08 ± 6.76</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">4.31</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot><p><sup>a</sup>Inhibition rate was determined at the concentration of 20 µM.</p></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap></p><p id="Par13">Considering the reported broad-spectrum antimicrobial activity of many cannabinoids, we aimed to assess the antimicrobial potential of compounds <bold>1–4</bold>. Three Gram-positive bacteria (<italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">Bacillus subtilis</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">Micrococcus luteus</italic>), one Gram-negative bacteria (<italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic>), and one fungus (<italic toggle="yes">Trichophyton rubrum</italic>) were recruited. As shown in Table <xref rid="Tab2" ref-type="table">2</xref>, compounds <bold>1–4</bold> exhibited specific inhibitory activities against Gram-positive bacteria but were inactive against <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">T. rubrum</italic>. The EC<sub>50</sub> values of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> against <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic> ranged from 0.87 to 35.13 µM. The substrate OA showed no activity, emphasizing the importance of prenylation for antibacterial activity. Notably, compound <bold>4</bold> exhibited the most potent antibacterial activity among these cannabinoid analogs, with EC<sub>50</sub> values of 0.87–3.16 µM, suggesting that the C<sub>15</sub> farnesyl group at the C-5 position of OA also contributed to the antibacterial property against Gram-positive bacteria.<table-wrap id="Tab2" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Table 2</label><caption><p>Antimicrobial effects of compounds <bold>1–4</bold></p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><thead><tr><th rowspan="2" colspan="1">Microorganism</th><th colspan="6" rowspan="1">EC<sub>50</sub> (µM)</th></tr><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">1</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">2</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">3</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">4</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">OA</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">CK<sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">24.21 ± 2.95</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">4.93 ± 0.70</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">9.62 ± 1.45</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.87 ± 0.14</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.48 ± 0.05</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">12.50 ± 1.17</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">2.62 ± 0.14</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">5.34 ± 0.56</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">2.08 ± 0.12</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.21 ± 0.03</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">35.13 ± 3.78</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">8.85 ± 0.50</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">17.62 ± 1.36</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">3.16 ± 0.42</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.27 ± 0.03</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1"
