IR (KBr) <italic toggle="yes">ν</italic><sub>max</sub>: 3389, 2927, 1631, 1608, 1449, 1252, 1178 cm<sup>−1</sup>; UV (MeOH): <italic toggle="yes">λ</italic><sub>max</sub> 200 nm; HR-ESI-MS (<italic toggle="yes">m/z</italic>): [M-H]<sup>-</sup> calcd. for C<sub>27</sub>H<sub>39</sub>O<sub>4</sub>, 427.2854; found, 427.2851.</p></sec><sec id="Sec15"><title>Modeling and molecular docking simulations</title><p id="Par29">The alphafold model of AscC and AscC<sup>L180F</sup> was generated using web service (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" ext-link-type="uri" xlink:href="https://wemol.wecomput.com/">https://wemol.wecomput.com/</ext-link>). Hydrogen atoms and atom charges were subsequently added. The substrates FDP and OA undergoing structural optimization were docked into the binding pocket of AscC and AscC<sup>L180F</sup> using the AutoDockTools-1.5.6. The docking results were then visualized and analyzed by PyMOL.</p></sec><sec id="Sec16"><title>Site-directed mutagenesis of AscC</title><p id="Par30">Alanine scanning was used to identify candidate residues with a Radius within 3 Å, while affinity maturation was employed to design mutants with a focus on those exhibiting a binding energy change exceeding 0.4. Mutants were constructed using overlap extension PCR and then subcloned into the pET28a vector. The primers were listed in Supplementary Table <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">2</xref>. Each mutant was confirmed by DNA sequencing. Afterward, the resultant variants were transformed into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> BL21 (DE3) harboring pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS.</p></sec><sec id="Sec17"><title>Qualitative analysis of the products in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains</title><p id="Par31">Calibration curves of compounds <bold>1,</bold> <bold>2</bold>, and <bold>4</bold> were generated using HPLC following the method described above. Five concentration gradients of the isolated compound <bold>1</bold> (80, 40, 20, 10, 5 µg/mL), compound <bold>2</bold> (60, 30, 15, 7.5, 3.75 µg/mL) and compound <bold>4</bold> (500, 250, 125, 62.5, 31.25 µg/mL) were prepared. Each concentration was injected in triplicate, and the linearity of the standard curve was determined by plotting the peak area versus concentration. The resulting equation and correlation coefficient obtained from the linearity study were y = 19252x + 55.984 (R<sup>2</sup> = 0.9981) for compound <bold>1</bold>, y = 13688x + 43.024 (R<sup>2</sup> = 0.9973) for compound <bold>2</bold>, and y = 15153x + 192.93 (R<sup>2</sup> = 0.9993) for compound <bold>4</bold>. The yields of compound <bold>3</bold> were calculated according to the calibration curve of compound <bold>4</bold>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec18"><title>Anti-inflammatory activity assay of compounds 1–4</title><p id="Par32">The assay was performed according to the literature with certain modifications<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR43">43</xref></sup>. Briefly, BV-2 microglia cells were cultured in DMEM high-glucose medium containing 10% fetal bovine serum, penicillin (100 U/mL), and streptomycin (100 µg/mL) at 37 °C in a humidified atmosphere with 5% CO<sub>2</sub> and 95% air. BV-2 cells (2×10<sup>5</sup> cells/well) were seeded in 96-well plates and cultured overnight until reaching 70–90% confluence. Subsequently, the cells were pretreated with different concentrations of test compounds (80, 40, 20, 10, and 5 µM) for 1 h, followed by exposure to 1 µg/mL of LPS for 24 h. The levels of TNF-α and IL-6 in the culture supernatants were assessed using enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) kits following the manufacturer’s protocol (BD Biosciences). The absorbance value (A) of each well at 450 nm (570 nm calibration) was measured with a microplate reader (Molecular Devices). Inhibitory rates of TNF-α and IL-6 production were calculated according to the following formula: Inhibitory rate = (1 – (A<sub>sample</sub> – A<sub>blank</sub>)/(A<sub>solvent</sub> – A<sub>blank</sub>)) × 100%. IC<sub>50</sub> values were analyzed by log (inhibitor) vs. normalized response – Variable slope using Prism 9.0 software. Each experiment was conducted with three independent biological replicates.</p></sec><sec id="Sec19"><title>Antimicrobial activity assay of compounds 1–4</title><p id="Par33"><italic toggle="yes">T. rubrum</italic> ATCC4438 was bought from Hospital for Skin Diseases, Institute of Dermatology Chinese of Medical Sciences, Peking Union Medical College. <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> ATCC25922 was bought from HuanKai Microbial Co., Ltd. <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic> were bought from Institute of Resource Insects, Chinese Academy of Forestry Sciences. The antimicrobial assays of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> were conducted using a micro-broth dilution method in 96-well culture plates according to the Clinical and Laboratory Standards Institute (CLSI). Compounds <bold>1–4</bold> were prepared as a 20 mM stock solution in dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO). Ampicillin sodium and cefotaxime sodium were used as the positive agents for the antibacterial assay of Gram-positive bacteria and Gram-negative bacteria, respectively, while terbinafine hydrochloride was used as the positive agent for antifungal activity. The four bacterial strains were cultured on LB agar plates, whereas the fungal strain was cultured on the potato-dextrose agar (PDA) plates. The colonies were taken from the agar plates and incubated in the corresponding medium. The microbe suspension was deliquated with fresh LB or potato-dextrose broth (PDB) to 10<sup>5</sup> cfu/mL. The diluted suspensions were then transferred to a 96-well plate (200 µL per well) containing different concentrations of the test compound. Serial dilutions of the stock solution were performed with final concentrations ranging from 50 to 0.39 µg/mL (including 50, 25, 12.5, 6.25, 3.125, 1.5625, 0.78125, 0.390625 µg/mL). The microplates were incubated at 37 °C for 24 h for the bacteria or at 28 °C for 96 h for the fungi. All assays were performed in quardruplicate. EC<sub>50</sub> was defined as the minimum concentration inhibiting 50% of microbial growth and was calculated using GraphPad Prism 9.0 software.</p></sec><sec id="Sec20"><title>Statistics and reproducibility</title><p id="Par34">We calculated the compound yields synthesized by the engineered strains using peak areas and the calibration curves derived from purified compounds. The compound yields for each strain were measured in triplicate biological replicates, with each replicate originating from different positive clones.</p></sec><sec id="Sec21"><title>Reporting summary</title><p id="Par35">Further information on research design is available in the <xref rid="MOESM4" ref-type="media">Nature Portfolio Reporting Summary</xref> linked to this article.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec22" sec-type="supplementary-material"><title>Supplementary information</title><p> <supplementary-material content-type="local-data" id="MOESM1" position="float" orientation="portrait"><media xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_MOESM1_ESM.pdf" position="float" orientation="portrait"><?suppdata-name 42003_2025_7509_MOESM1_ESM.pdf?><?suppdata-size 2560724?><?suppdata-md5 b937e358e4140dc047efed6947c01f00?><?suppdata-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?suppdata-mime-type application?><?suppdata-mime-sub-type pdf?><?suppdata-cloudpmc-urn urn:app:0f1f/11794439/b937e358e414/42003_2025_7509_MOESM1_ESM.pdf?><caption><p>Supplementary Information</p></caption></media></supplementary-material>
- **assay**: <graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="anchor" id="d33e246" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 46447?><?image-md5 87c6217a421b7c4bb939bc9c03e01fb2?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 467?><?image-original-width 950?><?image-scaled-height 311?><?image-scaled-width 633?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/87c6217a421b/42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 5423?><?thumb-md5 8294375f765c9425c4f449aa4790a9fd?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 80?><?thumb-scaled-width 162?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/8294375f765c/42003_2025_7509_Figa_HTML.gif?></graphic> </p></abstract><abstract id="Abs2" abstract-type="web-summary"><p id="Par3">Four cannabinoid derivatives are biosynthesized in engineered bacteria. Two mutants are engineered to selectively produce an undescribed compound with a C15 prenyl chain at the C-5 position and antineuroinflammatory and antibacterial activities.</p></abstract><kwd-group kwd-group-type="npg-subject"><title>Subject terms</title><kwd>Enzyme mechanisms</kwd><kwd>Biocatalysis</kwd></kwd-group><funding-group><award-group><funding-source><institution-wrap><institution-id institution-id-type="FundRef">https://doi.org/10.13039/501100001809</institution-id><institution>National Natural Science Foundation of China (National Science Foundation of China)</institution></institution-wrap></funding-source><award-id>82222072</award-id><principal-award-recipient><name name-style="western"><surname>Liu</surname><given-names>Yan</given-names></name></principal-award-recipient></award-group></funding-group><custom-meta-group><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-qastatus</meta-name><meta-value>0</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-live</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-embargo</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-released</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-open-access</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-olf</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-manuscript</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-legally-suppressed</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-has-pdf</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-has-supplement</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-pdf-only</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-suppress-copyright</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-is-real-version</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-is-scanned-article</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-preprint</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-in-epmc</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-license-ref</meta-name><meta-value>CC BY-NC-ND</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>issue-copyright-statement</meta-name><meta-value>© Springer Nature Limited 2025</meta-value></custom-meta></custom-meta-group></article-meta></front><body><sec id="Sec1" sec-type="introduction"><title>Introduction</title><p id="Par4">Natural products play pivotal roles in chemical biology and drug discovery due to their unique chemical structures and specific biological targets<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR1">1</xref></sup>. The construction of natural product analogs is important for exploring structure-activity relationships, developing drugs with enhanced bioactivity and bioavailability, and uncovering disease mechanisms as chemical biology tools<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR2">2</xref></sup>. With the advances in synthetic biology, combinatorial biosynthesis exploiting enzymatic and pathway promiscuity provides an alternative approach to generate novel natural product analogs<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR2">2</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR5">5</xref></sup>.</p><p id="Par5">Meroterpenoids represent a class of natural products with conspicuous biological activities derived from the terpenoid biosynthetic pathway coupled with other biogenic pathways. Among these, cannabinoids stand out as unique meroterpenoids initially isolated from the plant <italic toggle="yes">Cannabis sativa</italic>, a herbal medicine with a history spanning over 3500 years<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR6">6</xref></sup>. More than 120 phytocannabinoids have been isolated from <italic toggle="yes">Cannabis</italic>, most of which are alkyl resorcinol derivatives with a monoterpenoid unit<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR7">7</xref></sup>. Notably, cannabinoids, such as Δ<sup>9</sup>-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) and cannabidiol (CBD), exhibit potent pharmacological effects, including analgesic, antiemetic, anti-spasmodic, antimicrobial, and anti-inflammatory activities<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR8">8</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR10">10</xref></sup>, which renders them of great medicinal importance<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR11">11</xref></sup>. The pharmacological activities of cannabinoids principally occur through interacting with cannabinoid receptors CB1 and CB2, belonging to the superfamily of G-protein coupled receptors which mediate most of physiological responses and so have great potential as therapeutic targets for a broad spectrum of diseases<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR12">12</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR13">13</xref></sup>. The important influence of the C-3 side chain of cannabinoids on the pharmacological activities has been identified through chemical synthesis of a series of C-3 alkyl analogs<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR14">14</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR16">16</xref></sup>, however, the effect of the prenyl chain on pharmaceutical properties remains unexplored.</p><p id="Par6">The biosynthesis of cannabinoids is derived from a monoterpenoid unit produced by 2-<italic toggle="yes">C</italic>-methyl-D-erythritol 4-phosphate (MEP) pathway and an aromatic moiety olivetolic acid (OA) catalyzed by an olivetol synthase and an OA cyclase using malonyl-CoA and hexanoyl-CoA as substrates<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR17">17</xref></sup>. Cannabigerolic acid (CBGA) is the committed biosynthetic precursor of cannabinoids, formed by a C-C Friedel-Craft alkylation of OA with geranyl diphosphate (C<sub>10</sub>, GDP)<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR18">18</xref></sup>. Subsequently, the geranyl moiety of CBGA undergoes cyclization reaction to form cannabinoids such as Δ<sup>9</sup>-tetrahydrocannabinolic acid (THCA), cannabidiolic acid (CBDA), or cannabichromenic acid (CBCA), which can be decarboxylated to their neutral forms (Fig. <xref rid="Fig1" ref-type="fig">1</xref>)<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR19">19</xref></sup>.<fig id="Fig1" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 1</label><caption><p>The biosynthetic pathway of cannabinoids.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e348" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 76817?><?image-md5 eebf2bdab2c685ba9ed413d6cb2d1e30?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1463?><?image-original-width 1749?><?image-scaled-height 585?><?image-scaled-width 699?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/eebf2bdab2c6/42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 2956?><?thumb-md5 6f7d19eca7bba26f14b9d37584c1bafd?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 84?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/6f7d19eca7bb/42003_2025_7509_Fig1_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p><p id="Par7">Heterologous biosynthesis of cannabinoids has been achieved in engineered yeasts, paving an alternative way for access to cannabinoids<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR22">22</xref></sup>. Based on enzyme promiscuity and engineered biosynthetic pathways, a series of unnatural cannabinoid analogs with distinct alkyl moieties at the C-3 position were obtained by combinatorial biosynthesis through feeding different fatty acids<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR23">23</xref></sup>. Given the diversity of terpenoids which includes hemiterpenoids, monoterpenoids, sesquiterpenoids, diterpenoids, and sesterterpenoids, we are particularly interested in the biosynthesis of CBGA analogs with distinct terpenoid chains. Several aromatic prenyltransferases have been reported, among which AscC, a UbiA-type prenyltransferase from <italic toggle="yes">Fusarium</italic> sp. NBRC 100844, could catalyze the conversion of 2-methyl-4,6-dihydroxy benzaldehyde into the corresponding farnesylated product<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR24">24</xref></sup>. In this work, we synthesized four CBGA analogs <bold>1–4</bold> with a C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, or C<sub>15</sub> prenyl unit using the engineered <italic toggle="yes">Escherichia coli</italic> harboring the AscC gene. Molecular docking and site-directed mutagenesis resulted in two mutants selectively producing the undescribed compoud <bold>4</bold>. In addition, the anti-neuroinflammatory and antibacterial activities of these compounds were observed. </p></sec><sec id="Sec2" sec-type="results"><title>Results</title><sec id="Sec3"><title>Heterologous expression of promiscuous aromatic prenyltransferases in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains for cannabinoid analogs production</title><p id="Par8">To biosynthesize cannabinoid analogs featuring diverse prenyl side chains, the engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains were recruited, which contain an exogenous MVA pathway along with a farnesyl diphosphate synthase (FDPS) or geranylgeranyl diphosphate (GGDPS) or geranylfarnesyl diphosphate synthase (GFDPS) previously constructed for terpenoid biosynthesis<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR25">25</xref></sup>. The aromatic prenyltransferase (CsPT4) from <italic toggle="yes">C. sativa</italic> and four fungal promiscuous aromatic prenyltransferases (aPTases), including AscC from <italic toggle="yes">Fusarium</italic> sp., Trt2 and AtaPT from <italic toggle="yes">Aspergillus terreus</italic>, and Orf2 from <italic toggle="yes">Streptomyces</italic> sp.<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR24">24</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR26">26</xref>–<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR29">29</xref></sup>. were picked out to test their activity for transferring various prenyl groups to the aromatic ring of OA (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">1</xref>). The full-length cDNA of these aPTases was synthesized after codon optimization and then constructed into the corresponding expression vector.</p><p id="Par9">The four fungal aromatic prenyltransferases could express substantial amounts of recombinant proteins with expected molecular mass in the soluble fraction. These aPTases were subsequently transformed into the engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains producing C<sub>5</sub>-C<sub>25</sub> prenyl diphosphates. By feeding with OA, four specific peaks could be detected in the extract of engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> containing AscC using high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) and liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS) analysis (Fig. <xref rid="Fig2" ref-type="fig">2</xref>). The pseudo-molecular ion peaks ([M-H]<sup>–</sup>) of specific products were 291.1601, 359.2226, 427.2853, and 427.2851, indicating that AscC could catalyze the introduction of C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, and C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chains into OA (Fig. <xref rid="Fig2" ref-type="fig">2</xref>). The conversion rate of OA was 70.48% by AscC. By contrast, no obvious product was observed in the engineered bacteria harboring Trt2, AtaPT, and Orf2. In addition, we tested the catalytic activity of AscC towards a series of OA analogs (<bold>S1</bold>-<bold>S7</bold>), but no products were observed (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">2</xref>). Compared to OA, these analogs lack a hydroxyl group at the C-4 position, except <bold>S4</bold> that has an ester group at the C-1 position instead of a carboxyl group. These results suggest that both the C-4 hydroxyl group and the C-1 carboxyl group in the substrate are important for enzyme activity of AscC.<fig id="Fig2" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 2</label><caption><title>The conversion of olivetolic acid (OA) to prenylation products by engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC.</title><p><bold>A</bold> HPLC analysis of the extract of engineered bacteria harboring pET28a/AscC (a) and empty vector pET28a (b). <bold>B</bold> The mass spectrometry of peaks 1–4.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e488" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 72821?><?image-md5 f0bee20117c83f243026ffbd2ed347c3?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1048?><?image-original-width 1349?><?image-scaled-height 524?><?image-scaled-width 674?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/f0bee20117c8/42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 2943?><?thumb-md5 7cc1786ba170d159b4e3f726e87027fc?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 79?><?thumb-scaled-width 102?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/7cc1786ba170/42003_2025_7509_Fig2_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p></sec><sec id="Sec4"><title>Identification of cannabinoid analogs produced by AscC in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></title><p id="Par10">The plasmid pET28a/AscC was transferred into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strain BL21 harboring the pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS plasmid, which contains a complete gene set of the mevalonate (MVA) pathway and a FDPS gene<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR30">30</xref></sup>, resulting in an engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strain BPA1. To elucidate the structure of cannabinoid analogs, a 10 L scale fermentation of the engineered BPA1 strain was performed and extracted with ethyl acetate. The <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> extracts were further purified with a combination of repeated silica-gel and Sephadex LH-20 column chromatography. Afterward, semi-preparative HPLC was carried out using a mixture of acetonitrile and acid water as a mobile phase to afford compounds <bold>1–4</bold>. Notably, compound <bold>4</bold> emerged as the predominant component (Fig. <xref rid="Fig2" ref-type="fig">2</xref>).</p><p id="Par11">Compound <bold>4</bold> was obtained as a yellowish powder and characterized by a molecular formula of C<sub>27</sub>H<sub>40</sub>O<sub>4</sub> with eight degrees of unsaturation based on high-resolution electron ionization mass spectroscopy (HR-EI-MS) (<italic toggle="yes">m/z</italic> 427.2851 [M-H]<sup>-</sup>, calcd. 427.2854) and NMR spectroscopic data (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">3</xref>–<xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">10</xref>). Compound <bold>4</bold> showed the same molecular weight as compound <bold>3</bold> but a different retention time, implying that they might be prenylated products of OA at different positions. Compound <bold>3</bold> was determined as a cannabinoid analog 2,4-dihydroxy-3-[(2<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>,6<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>)-3,7,11-trimethyl-2,6,10-dodecatrien-1-yl]-6-pentylbenzoic acid through careful inspection of the NMR data (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">11</xref>, <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">12</xref>) and comparison with those reported in the literature<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR31">31</xref></sup>. The 1D and 2D NMR spectra of <bold>4</bold> closely resembled those of <bold>3</bold> with a key difference being the upfield movement of the carbon at C-3 (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 102.2) and C-1′′ (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 31.5) in <bold>4</bold> compared to <bold>3</bold> (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 111.1 for C-3 and <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 36.7 for C-1′′). Additionally, the carbon at C-5 (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 119.9) and C-1′ (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 25.1) in <bold>4</bold> moved downfield compared to <bold>3</bold> (<italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 111.6 for C-5 and <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic><sub>C</sub> 22.2 for C-1′) indicating a C<sub>15</sub> farnesyl chain attached to C-5. Confirmation of this attachment was provided by the <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H correlation spectroscopy (COSY) correlations of H-1′/H-2′, H-4′/H-5′/H-6′ as well as heteronuclear multiple bond correlation (HMBC) correlations of H-1′ with C-4/C-5/C-6/C-2′/C-3′ (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). The <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H COSY correlations of H-1′′/H-2′′/H-3′′/H-4′′/H-5′′, combined with the HMBC correlations of H-1′′ with C-1/C-5/C-6, provided evidence for the attachment of the <italic toggle="yes">n</italic>-pentyl chain to C-6 and confirmed the planar structure of <bold>4</bold> (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). Accordingly, the structure of <bold>4</bold> was determined as 2,4-dihydroxy-5-[(2<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>,6<italic toggle="yes">E</italic>)-3,7,11-trimethyl-2,6,10-dodecatrien-1-yl]-6-pentylbenzoic acid (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). The structures of compounds <bold>1</bold> and <bold>2</bold> were identified as 2,4-dihydroxy-3-(3-methylbut-2-en-1-yl)-6-pentylbenzoic acid<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR32">32</xref></sup> and CBGA<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR33">33</xref></sup> (Supplementary Figs. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">13</xref>–<xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">16</xref>), respectively, through comparison of their NMR data with those reported in the literature (Fig. <xref rid="Fig3" ref-type="fig">3</xref>). Compounds <bold>1–4</bold> are cannabinoid analogs, in which compound <bold>4</bold> is an undescribed compound. The results indicated that engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC could produce four cannabinoid analogs with a C<sub>5</sub>, C<sub>10</sub>, or C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chain, likely attributed to the spacious hydrophobic substrate-binding pocket of promiscuous aPTases<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR34">34</xref></sup>.<fig id="Fig3" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 3</label><caption><p>Chemical structures of the products produced by engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC and key <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H COSY and HMBC correlations of compound <bold>4</bold>.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e719" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 46529?><?image-md5 05d6f657d194190abae46921a77896e5?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 775?><?image-original-width 2000?><?image-scaled-height 310?><?image-scaled-width 800?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/05d6f657d194/42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 3408?><?thumb-md5 c6f5520d394ebf732b42cef5eb15f8c0?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 78?><?thumb-scaled-width 200?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/c6f5520d394e/42003_2025_7509_Fig3_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p></sec><sec id="Sec5"><title>Compounds 1–4 displayed potent anti-inflammatory and antibacterial activities</title><p id="Par12">Cannabinoids are known for their potent anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive properties, likely through interacting with G protein-coupled receptors that are closely related to immune regulation and neurodegeneration<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR12">12</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR13">13</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR35">35</xref></sup>. Therefore, four cannabinoid analogs were evaluated for their anti-neuroinflammatory activity by investigating the inhibition of TNF-α and IL-6 secretion in BV-2 microglial cells. As shown in Table <xref rid="Tab1" ref-type="table">1</xref>, compound <bold>4</bold> showed potent inhibitory effects on TNF-α and IL-6 secretion, with IC<sub>50</sub> values of 3.06 and 4.31 µM, respectively. Compounds <bold>1–3</bold> showed moderate activity against TNF-α and IL-6 secretion with 34.29–48.17% inhibition rates at 20 µM. By comparing the structures and activity of compounds <bold>1–4</bold>, it can be concluded that the C<sub>15</sub> farnesyl unit at the C-5 position of OA played an important role in anti-inflammatory activity.<table-wrap id="Tab1" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Table 1</label><caption><p>Inhibitory effects of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> on TNF-α and IL-6 secretion of BV-2 microglial cells induced by LPS</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><thead><tr><th rowspan="2" colspan="1">Compound</th><th colspan="3" rowspan="1">TNF-α secretion</th><th colspan="2" rowspan="1">IL-6 secretion</th></tr><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibition rate (%)<sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></th><th colspan="2" rowspan="1">IC<sub>50</sub> (µM)</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">Inhibition rate (%)<sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">IC<sub>50</sub> (µM)</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>1</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">34.29 ± 6.84</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">35.01 ± 5.94</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>2</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">40.62 ± 7.93</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">48.17 ± 5.57</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>3</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">41.20 ± 6.36</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">46.21 ± 5.84</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">–</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><bold>4</bold></td><td colspan="2" rowspan="1">85.35 ± 7.32</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">3.06</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">81.08 ± 6.76</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">4.31</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot><p><sup>a</sup>Inhibition rate was determined at the concentration of 20 µM.</p></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap></p><p id="Par13">Considering the reported broad-spectrum antimicrobial activity of many cannabinoids, we aimed to assess the antimicrobial potential of compounds <bold>1–4</bold>. Three Gram-positive bacteria (<italic toggle="yes">Staphylococcus aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">Bacillus subtilis</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">Micrococcus luteus</italic>), one Gram-negative bacteria (<italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic>), and one fungus (<italic toggle="yes">Trichophyton rubrum</italic>) were recruited. As shown in Table <xref rid="Tab2" ref-type="table">2</xref>, compounds <bold>1–4</bold> exhibited specific inhibitory activities against Gram-positive bacteria but were inactive against <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">T. rubrum</italic>. The EC<sub>50</sub> values of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> against <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic> ranged from 0.87 to 35.13 µM. The substrate OA showed no activity, emphasizing the importance of prenylation for antibacterial activity. Notably, compound <bold>4</bold> exhibited the most potent antibacterial activity among these cannabinoid analogs, with EC<sub>50</sub> values of 0.87–3.16 µM, suggesting that the C<sub>15</sub> farnesyl group at the C-5 position of OA also contributed to the antibacterial property against Gram-positive bacteria.<table-wrap id="Tab2" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Table 2</label><caption><p>Antimicrobial effects of compounds <bold>1–4</bold></p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><thead><tr><th rowspan="2" colspan="1">Microorganism</th><th colspan="6" rowspan="1">EC<sub>50</sub> (µM)</th></tr><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">1</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">2</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">3</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">4</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">OA</th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1">CK<sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">24.21 ± 2.95</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">4.93 ± 0.70</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">9.62 ± 1.45</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.87 ± 0.14</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.48 ± 0.05</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">12.50 ± 1.17</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">2.62 ± 0.14</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">5.34 ± 0.56</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">2.08 ± 0.12</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.21 ± 0.03</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">35.13 ± 3.78</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">8.85 ± 0.50</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">17.62 ± 1.36</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">3.16 ± 0.42</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.27 ± 0.03</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">0.15 ± 0.02</td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">T. rubrum</italic></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">NA</td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1">3.38 ± 0.76</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot><p><sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup> The positive control for <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic> was ampicillin sodium. The positive control for <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> was cefotaxime sodium, and the positive control for <italic toggle="yes">T. rubrum</italic> was terbinafine hydrochloride.</p></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap></p></sec><sec id="Sec6"><title>Improved production of compounds 1–4 in <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></title><p id="Par14">We analyzed the production of four compounds in <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains carrying pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS and pET28a/AscC using an external standard method. HPLC analysis showed that the yields of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> were 0.12 ± 0.02 mg/L, 0.08 ± 0.01 mg/L, 0.38 ± 0.05 mg/L, and 7.31 ± 0.60 mg/L, respectively. Notably, a significant amount of unconsumed prenyl substrate FDP was observed in the engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic>, prompting an attempt to enhance the yield of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> by increasing the copy numbers of the AscC gene. For this purpose, an additional plasmid pCold-TF/AscC was transformed into the <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS and pET28a/AscC while the pCold-TF empty vector was co-transformed into the same strain as a negative control. HPLC analysis showed that the yields of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> significantly improved to 2.06 ± 0.10 mg/L, 1.63 ± 0.12 mg/L, 0.91 ± 0.09 mg/L and 14.85 ± 0.91 mg/L respectively in the strain containing two AscC plasmids, which were 17.85, 20.80, 2.42 and 2.03-fold of those in the strain containing plasmid pET28a/AscC, respectively (Fig. <xref rid="Fig4" ref-type="fig">4</xref>). The results indicated that increasing AscC copy numbers led to a substantial increase in the yields of compounds <bold>1–4</bold>. Different yields of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> may be attributed to the availability of various prenyl diphosphates, as FDP was the predominant product in the engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> containing the pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS plasmid. However, the production of compounds <bold>1</bold> and <bold>2</bold> showed a significantly higher increase compared to compounds <bold>3</bold> and <bold>4</bold> after introducing an additional copy of AscC, suggesting that the enzymatic efficiency of AscC has a greater impact on the product profiles than the availability of prenyl diphosphates. Although prenylation at either the C-3 or C-5 position of OA occurs when using FDP as a prenyl donor, the yield of compound <bold>4</bold> was significantly higher than that of compound <bold>3</bold>. These results suggest that prenylation reactions catalyzed by AscC exhibit a regioselective preference for the <italic toggle="yes">ortho</italic> position of the <italic toggle="yes">n</italic>-pentyl group over the two electron-donating hydroxyl groups on the aromatic ring.<fig id="Fig4" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 4</label><caption><title>The yields of compounds 1–4 in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring AscC. Co-transformation pET28a/AscC and pCold-TF empty vector in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> containing the pBbA5c-MevT-of MBIS plasmid served as a control for determining the effect of AscC gene copy on product yields. Four biologically independent experiments were performed.</title><p>The error bars represent the standard deviation (S.D.) of the mean.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e1138" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig4_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig4_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 38090?><?image-md5 dce78902c3cbfa2fedcb59204e7cb84a?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 661?><?image-original-width 950?><?image-scaled-height 440?><?image-scaled-width 633?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/dce78902c3cb/42003_2025_7509_Fig4_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig4_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 2823?><?thumb-md5 aaaeba5cf631a861295ca110435bcc73?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 79?><?thumb-scaled-width 114?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/aaaeba5cf631/42003_2025_7509_Fig4_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p></sec><sec id="Sec7"><title>Engineering AscC for selective production of compound 4</title><p id="Par15">Given that four different cannabinoid analogs are produced by AscC, we are motivated to engineer the enzyme to improve its product specificity. The AscC protein model contained a sizable central cavity capable of accommodating diverse prenyl substrates (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5A</xref>). In the model of AscC docked with structurally geometry-optimized FDP and OA, we found 14 amino acid residues interacting with the bound substrates (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5B</xref>). The residues Y162, K166, and D226 located in the conserved motifs (Y<sub>162</sub>XXXK<sub>166</sub> and D<sub>226</sub>XXXD<sub>230</sub>) and R110 interacted with the pyrophosphate group of FDP were excluded because of their crucial roles for enzyme activity<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR36">36</xref></sup>. Then, computational alanine scanning on the residues within 3 Å of OA or FDP was performed, and the amino acids with binding energy exceeding 0.4 were selected as candidates for further investigation (Supplementary Table <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">1</xref>). The in silico prediction showed that mutating V215 to alanine would result in the destabilization of the protein when FDP served as the lignan. Mutating D219 and L176 to alanine was predicted to increase protein stability and lead to substantial energy alterations, but mutation of D219A would disrupt the essential hydrogen bond between D219 and FDP. Mutating Y309 to alanine would lead to a significant change in binding energy and enhance protein stability, but the π-alkyl interaction between Y309 and FDP would be disrupted. Mutations L180A and Y222A exhibited a neutral effect and moderate energy changes. Therefore, we obtained six variants by site-directed mutagenesis, including L176F, L176Y, L180F, L180Y, Y222L, and Y222F, based on ΔΔG changes to improve the thermal stability of the protein. These mutants were individually transformed into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> harboring the pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS plasmid.<fig id="Fig5" position="float" orientation="portrait"><label>Fig. 5</label><caption><title>Analysis of crucial residues in AscC.</title><p><bold>A</bold> Overview of AscC model docked with FDP and OA. <bold>B</bold> Interactions between the substrates and individual residues in AscC. Yellow, blue, red, green, orange, violet, and black dash lines represent hydrogen bonds, alkyl interactions, π-alkyl interactions, carbon interactions, charge interactions, π-cation interactions, and donor-donor interactions, respectively. The visualization was generated using Pymol. <bold>C</bold> Compound yields of wild-type AscC and its mutants expressed in the pET28a vector in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> containing the pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS plasmid. **<italic toggle="yes">P</italic> &lt; 0.01vs WT. Five biologically independent experiments were performed. The error bars represent the standard deviation (S.D.) of the mean. <bold>D</bold>–<bold>G</bold> Interactions between the substrates and residues in AscC and its mutant L180F.</p></caption><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" id="d33e1197" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_Fig5_HTML.jpg"><?image-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig5_HTML.jpg?><?image-size 156263?><?image-md5 0510e8ffbd7eada7ba0a0e4b7cbb566b?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1822?><?image-original-width 1999?><?image-scaled-height 728?><?image-scaled-width 799?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/0510e8ffbd7e/42003_2025_7509_Fig5_HTML.jpg?><?thumb-name 42003_2025_7509_Fig5_HTML.gif?><?thumb-size 5112?><?thumb-md5 2288821d89d30f76121d4ac709f97bf7?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 91?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0f1f/11794439/2288821d89d3/42003_2025_7509_Fig5_HTML.gif?></graphic></fig></p><p id="Par16">Interestingly, the variants L180Y and L180F selectively utilized FDP as a prenyl donor and specifically catalyzed the isopentenyl reaction of OA at the C-5 position to produce compound <bold>4</bold> (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5C</xref>). We constructed the protein model of L180F using AlphaFold 2 and docked the substrates OA and FDP into the binding pocket. OA was observed to exhibit different conformations in the wild-type and mutant enzymes (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5E</xref>). In L180F, there were more interactions between the residues R41, G47, and R90 and the carboxyl or 2-hydroxyl group of OA (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5F, G</xref>), which likely reduced the binding flexibility of OA within the pocket. Moreover, a π-alkyl interaction between Y222 and the alkyl side chain of OA in L180F replaced the alkyl-alkyl interaction between M50 and the alkyl side chain of OA in AscC. Additional π-alkyl interactions between OA and FDP were also observed. Accordingly, the positioning of OA was likely stabilized within the enzyme pocket, facilitating regioselective Friedel-Craft alkylation at C-5 (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5D</xref>). In addition, the yields of compound <bold>4</bold> in variants L180Y and L180F reached up to 11.71 and 12.95 mg/L, respectively. The switch from an alkyl-alkyl to a π-alkyl interaction between the amino acid at position 180 and FDP, coupled with the additional π-alkyl interaction between Y218 and FDP, may provide enhanced cation stabilization. Besides, the reduced distance between C-5 and C-1 of FDP (from 6.7 to 6.2 Å) might also contribute to the improved conversion efficiency in variants L180Y and L180F. Furthermore, the natural substrates 2-methyl-4,6-dihydroxy benzaldehyde and FDP were also docked into the wild-type and mutant enzymes, respectively. The results suggested that L180F might still catalyze Friedel-Craft alkylation at the C-3 position, probably attributed to the methyl group at the C-1 position facilitating substrate flexibility within the enzyme pocket (Supplementary Fig. <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">17</xref>). However, the experiment was not conducted due to the unavailability of the substrate.</p><p id="Par17">The variants L176Y and L176F resulted in a substantial loss of enzymatic activity (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5C</xref>). Y222 interacted with FDP via π-alkyl interactions, and the mutants Y222L and Y222F exhibited a dramatic reduction in enzymatic activity, with 21% and 0.4% production of wild-type AscC, respectively. The residues R41, G47, and R90 were predicted to form hydrogen bonds or carbon interaction with OA, suggesting they play an important role in the binding and stabilizing of OA. This was confirmed by the substantial loss of enzymatic activity upon mutation R90E (Fig. <xref rid="Fig5" ref-type="fig">5C</xref>). The results highlighted the importance of these residues in enzyme catalysis.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec8" sec-type="discussion"><title>Discussion</title><p id="Par18">The development of natural product analogs holds significant promise in chemical biology and drug discovery. Cannabinoids represent a unique class of natural products with substantial pharmaceutical values. Notable efforts have focused on the generation of cannabinoid analogs with distinct alkyl moieties, such as combinatorial biosynthesis of unnatural cannabinoid analogs through feeding different fatty acids<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR23">23</xref></sup>. In this study, cannabinoid analogs with varied terpenoid chains were created by introducing a promiscuous fungal aromatic prenyltransferase AscC into engineered bacteria strains capable of terpenoid production. The most abundant product, compound <bold>4</bold>, is an undescribed compound featuring a C<sub>15</sub> prenyl chain at the C-5 position of olivetolic acid.</p><p id="Par19">Considering the steric hindrance of the <italic toggle="yes">n</italic>-pentyl group and the electron-donating effect of the hydroxyl groups in the aromatic ring, prenylation of OA would theoretically favor the C-3 position over the C-5 position. However, when using farnesyl diphosphate (FDP) as the prenyl donor, the amount of compound <bold>4</bold> prenylated at the C-5 position was significantly higher than that of compound <bold>3</bold> at the C-3 position. Notably, C-5 substituted cannabinoids are rare, with the exception of 5-geranyl olivetolate isolated from <italic toggle="yes">Helichrysum umbraculigerum</italic><sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR37">37</xref></sup>. A number of aPTases responsible for catalyzing the attachment of prenyl groups to aromatic substrates have been reported. For instance, UbiA-type PTases CsPT1 and CsPT4 reported from <italic toggle="yes">Cannabis</italic> catalyze the attachment of a geranyl group to OA<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref></sup>, and NphB (formerly Orf2) identified from <italic toggle="yes">Streptomyces</italic> sp. strain CL190 could catalyze the attachment of a geranyl group to aromatic substrates<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR28">28</xref></sup>. Recently, the core cannabinoid biosynthetic enzymes have also been identified from <italic toggle="yes">H. umbraculigerum</italic>, including four aPTases HuPT1-4 among that HuPT4 (HuCBGAS4) geranylated the aromatic dihydrostilbenic acid<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR38">38</xref></sup>. Notably, these identified aPTases predominantly catalyze prenylation at the C-3 position of aromatic substrates<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR20">20</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR31">31</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR39">39</xref></sup>. By contrast, AscC can catalyze OA to produce farnesylated products at both the C-3 and C-5 positions, suggesting a distinct catalytic mechanism. The unusual regioselectivity of AscC warrants further investigation, and the rise of artificial intelligence and language modes will help enzyme engineering in future<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR40">40</xref></sup>. The unique structure of compound <bold>4</bold> presents significant challenges for regioselective chemical synthesis. Guided by structure-based enzyme engineering, we successfully improved the selectivity of AscC, and developed two variants, L180Y and L180F, which exclusively produce compound <bold>4</bold>. These findings provide promising catalysts for manipulating the production of compound <bold>4</bold> using synthetic biology.</p><p id="Par20">We found that compounds <bold>1–4</bold> exerted significant anti-neuroinflammatory and antimicrobial properties, with compound <bold>4</bold> being the most effective. This indicates that the prenyl side chains at the C-5 position of OA play crucial roles in these activities. Additionally, novel cannabinoids with remarkable bioactivities could potentially be generated via prenyl cyclization. For instance, CBD demonstrates enhanced anti-inflammatory and antimicrobial activity compared to CBGA<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR41">41</xref>,<xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR42">42</xref></sup>. Further efforts should focus on exploring more cyclases and expression systems to expand the chemical space of cannabinoids.</p></sec><sec id="Sec9"><title>Methods</title><sec id="Sec10"><title>General experimental procedures</title><p id="Par21">Column chromatography was performed on silica gel (200 − 300 mesh, Qingdao Dingkang Silicone Co., Ltd., P. R. China) and Sephadex LH-20 (20 − 100 µm, Amersham Pharmacia Biotech, Sweden). HPLC-DAD analysis was carried out on an Agilent 1260 Infinity II equipped with a Zorbax SB-C<sub>18</sub> (5 µM, 4.6 × 250 mm, 1 mL/min). Semi-preparative HPLC analysis was performed on a HITACHI Primaide series instrument with a Shimadzu Shim-pack GIST-C<sub>18</sub> column (5 µm, 10 × 250 mm, 3 mL/min). Mass spectra were obtained on a Waters Synapt XS spectrometer equipped with a Zorbax Extend-C<sub>18</sub> column (1.8 µm, 4.6 × 50 mm, 0.3 mL/min). NMR spectra were recorded on a Bruker AV-700 (700 MHz) spectrometer, with TMS as an internal standard. IR spectra were recorded on a Bruker Tensor-27 spectrometer with KBr pellets. Genes were synthesized by Tsingke Biotechnology Co., Ltd.. OA was purchased from J&amp;K Scientific Co., Ltd.. The ethyl acetate used for extraction was analytical grade and acetonitrile used for HPLC was chromatographic grade. Other reagents were analytical grade.</p></sec><sec id="Sec11"><title>Strains, plasmids and media</title><p id="Par22">All primers used in this study were listed in Supplementary Table <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">2</xref>. <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> DH5α was used for plasmid maintenance and <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> BL21 (DE3) was used for gene heterologous expression. Plasmid pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS was kindly provided by Prof. Tao Liu at Tianjin Institute of Industrial Biotechnology, Chinese Academy of Sciences. Codon-optimized CsPT4 from <italic toggle="yes">C. sativa</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">AscC</italic> from the fungus <italic toggle="yes">Fusarium</italic> sp., <italic toggle="yes">Trt2</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">AtaPT</italic> from <italic toggle="yes">Aspergillus terreus</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">Orf2</italic> from <italic toggle="yes">Streptomyces</italic> sp. were subcloned into <italic toggle="yes">NdeI</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">XhoI</italic> sites of expression vector pET28a using ClonExpress® Ultra One Step Cloning Kit (Vazyme Bio.), generating corresponding plasmids. Similarly, the cDNA of AscC was inserted into <italic toggle="yes">SalI</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">XhoI</italic> sites of expression vector pCold-TF, resulting in the plasmid pCold-TF/AscC. The recombinant plasmids were individually transformed into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> expression strain BL21(DE3) that harbored the pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS vector to generate the corresponding strains. pET28a empty vector was transformed into the same strain as a negative control. The plasmid pCold-TF/AscC was co-transformed with pET28a/AscC into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strain BL21 harboring pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS to generate the strain BPA3. pCold-TF empty vector was transformed into the same strain as a negative control (BPA4). All strains constructed in this study were listed in Supplementary Table <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">3</xref>. The culture media used for <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains were Luria-Bertani (LB) medium (10 g/L tryptone, 5 g/L yeast extract, 10 g/L NaCl) or Terrific Broth (TB) medium (12 g/L tryptone, 24 g/L yeast extract, 4 mL/L glycerol, 2.31 g/L KH<sub>2</sub>PO<sub>4</sub>, 12.54 g/L K<sub>2</sub>HPO<sub>4</sub>). Ampicillin sodium (100 µg/mL), chloramphenicol (34 µg/mL), and kanamycin (50 µg/mL) were supplemented to media for screening positive recombinant strains.</p></sec><sec id="Sec12"><title>Product assay of engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></title><p id="Par23">The colonies of strains constructed in this study were screened using LB solid plates containing corresponding antibiotics and further confirmed by colony-PCR. Seed cultures (10 mL) of confirmed transformants were grown overnight at 37 °C in TB medium supplemented with corresponding antibiotics. 1 mL of seed culture was inoculated into 50 mL fresh TB broth supplemented with the same antibiotics and cultured at 37 °C until the cultures reached the optical density (OD<sub>600</sub>) of 0.6–0.8. Subsequently, 50 µL of 0.3 mM isopropyl-<italic toggle="yes">β</italic>-D-thiogalactopyranoside (IPTG) was added to induce the expression of the target proteins at 16 °C for 16 h. Afterward, 0.25 mg substrates (OA, compound <bold>S1</bold>-<bold>S7</bold>: salicylic acid, acetylsalicylic acid, ginkgoneolic acid, 2,4-dihydroxy-6-pentylbenzoate, 2-hydroxy-6-methylbenzoic acid, 2-methoxy-4-methylbenzoic acid or 4-ethyl-3-hydroxybenzoic acid) dissolved in anhydrous ethanol (10 mg/mL) was added to the cultures which were incubated at 25 °C for 72 h. Subsequently, the cultures were extracted with ethyl acetate by ultrasound (100 mL× 3, 30 mins per time) and the solvents were then removed by rotary evaporators. The residues were dissolved in 1 mL methanol and filtered for HPLC and LC-MS analysis. The mobile phase was composed of acetonitrile (solvent A) and 0.2% formic acid in water (solvent B), at a flow rate of 1.0 mL/min and a column temperature of 30°C. The elution gradient was as follows: 0–40 min, 5–75% A; 40.01–50 min, 75–95% A; 50.01–55 min, 95% A (for OA); while 0–5 min, 5% A; 5.01–30 min, 5–100% A; 30.01–35 min, 100% A (for <bold>S1</bold>-<bold>S7</bold>). The injection volume was 20 µL. LC-MS was performed under the same condition of HPLC, while electrospray ionization mass spectrometry was used in the negative ion mode with a capillary voltage of 3500 V.</p></sec><sec id="Sec13"><title>Isolation and structural elucidation of compounds produced by engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic></title><p id="Par24">A total of 10 L engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> cells harboring pET28a-AscC and pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS were cultivated. The cultures were extracted three times with ethyl acetate and the organic phase was combined and concentrated in vacuo. The crude extract (7 g) was subjected to silica gel column chromatography and eluted with petroleum ether-EtOAc (100:0, 10:1, 5:1, 2:1 and 0:1, <italic toggle="yes">v</italic>/<italic toggle="yes">v</italic>) to obtain five fractions (Fr.1-Fr.5). Fr.3 (1.2 g) was subjected to Sephadex LH-20 and eluted with CH<sub>2</sub>Cl<sub>2</sub>/MeOH (1:1, <italic toggle="yes">v</italic>/<italic toggle="yes">v</italic>), and then purified by semipreparative HPLC using MeCN/H<sub>2</sub>O (1:1, <italic toggle="yes">v</italic>/<italic toggle="yes">v</italic>) as a mobile phase to yield compound <bold>1</bold> (6 mg, t<sub>R</sub> = 37.0 min), <bold>2</bold> (3 mg, t<sub>R</sub> = 44.8 min), <bold>3</bold> (2 mg, t<sub>R</sub> = 51.1 min) and <bold>4</bold> (32 mg, t<sub>R</sub> = 52.5 min). Afterward, the compounds were subjected to extensive NMR analysis.</p></sec><sec id="Sec14"><title>Analytical data of compounds 1–4</title><p id="Par25">Compound <bold>1</bold>: Yellowish powder; <sup>1</sup>H NMR (700 MHz, CD<sub>3</sub>OD): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 6.18 (s, 1H), 5.21 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 6.9 Hz, 1H), 3.26 (d, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.2 Hz, 2H), 2.88 (d, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.7 Hz, 2H), 1.76 (s, 3H), 1.65 (s, 3H), 1.55 (m, 2H), 1.33 (m, 4H), 0.90 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 6.7 Hz, 3H); <sup>13</sup>C NMR (150 MHz, CD<sub>3</sub>OD): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 175.8, 164.2, 160.5, 146.7, 131.6, 124.3, 113.8, 110.6, 104.5, 37.4, 33.3, 32.9, 26.0, 23.6, 22.9, 17.9, 14.4.</p><p id="Par26">Compound <bold>2</bold>: Yellowish powder; <sup>1</sup>H NMR (700 MHz, CDCl<sub>3</sub>): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 6.27 (s, 1H), 5.28 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 6.6 Hz, 1H), 5.05 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 6.4 Hz, 1H), 3.43 (d, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.1 Hz, 2H), 2.88 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.9 Hz, 2H), 2.11 (m, 2H), 2.06 (m, 2H), 1.81 (s, 3H), 1.67 (s, 3H), 1.59 (s, 3H), 1.56 (m, 2H), 1.33 (m, 4H), 0.89 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 6.7 Hz, 3H); <sup>13</sup>C NMR (150 MHz, CDCl<sub>3</sub>): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 175.4, 163.7, 160.6, 147.4, 139.4, 132.2, 123.9, 121.5, 111.6, 111.3, 103.4, 39.9, 36.7, 32.2, 31.6, 26.5, 25.8, 22.7, 22.2, 17.9, 16.4, 14.2.</p><p id="Par27">Compound <bold>3</bold>: Yellowish powder; <sup>1</sup>H NMR (700 MHz, CDCl<sub>3</sub>): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 6.24 (s, 1H), 5.27 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.2 Hz, 1H), 5.08 (m, 2H), 3.39 (m, 2H), 2.87 (m, 2H), 2.11 (m, 2H), 2.05 (m, 4H), 1.96 (m, 2H), 1.81 (s, 3H), 1.67 (s, 3H), 1.59 (s, 3H), 1.58 (s, 3H), 1.55 (m, 2H), 1.29 (m, 4H), 0.88 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.1 Hz, 3H); <sup>13</sup>C NMR (150 MHz, CDCl<sub>3</sub>): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 174.9, 163.6, 160.3, 147.2, 139.2, 135.7, 131.4, 124.5, 123.8, 121.5, 111.6, 111.1, 103.6, 39.9, 39.8, 36.7, 32.2, 31.6, 26.8, 26.5, 25.8, 22.7, 22.2, 17.8, 16.4, 16.2, 14.2.</p><p id="Par28">Compound <bold>4</bold>: Yellowish powder; [<italic toggle="yes">α</italic>]<sup>20</sup><sub>D</sub> = - 5.13 (c 0.04 M in MeOH); <sup>1</sup>H NMR (700 MHz, CDCl<sub>3</sub>): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 6.33 (s, 1H), 5.08 (m, 3H), 3.35 (d, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 6.6 Hz, 2H), 2.96 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 8.3 Hz, 2H), 2.11 (m, 2H), 2.04 (m, 4H), 1.96 (m, 2H), 1.81 (s, 3H), 1.67 (s, 3H), 1.59 (s, 6H), 1.54 (m, 2H), 1.37 (m, 4H), 0.92 (t, <italic toggle="yes">J</italic> = 7.1 Hz, 3H); <sup>13</sup>C NMR (150 MHz, CDCl<sub>3</sub>): <italic toggle="yes">δ</italic> 176.1, 163.9, 161.1, 147.4, 137.9, 135.6, 131.5, 124.5, 123.8, 122.4, 119.9, 104.7, 102.2, 39.8, 39.8, 32.6, 31.5, 31.0, 26.8, 26.5, 25.8, 25.1, 22.6, 17.8, 16.5, 16.2, 14.2 (see Supplementary Table <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">4</xref>); IR (KBr) <italic toggle="yes">ν</italic><sub>max</sub>: 3389, 2927, 1631, 1608, 1449, 1252, 1178 cm<sup>−1</sup>; UV (MeOH): <italic toggle="yes">λ</italic><sub>max</sub> 200 nm; HR-ESI-MS (<italic toggle="yes">m/z</italic>): [M-H]<sup>-</sup> calcd. for C<sub>27</sub>H<sub>39</sub>O<sub>4</sub>, 427.2854; found, 427.2851.</p></sec><sec id="Sec15"><title>Modeling and molecular docking simulations</title><p id="Par29">The alphafold model of AscC and AscC<sup>L180F</sup> was generated using web service (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" ext-link-type="uri" xlink:href="https://wemol.wecomput.com/">https://wemol.wecomput.com/</ext-link>). Hydrogen atoms and atom charges were subsequently added. The substrates FDP and OA undergoing structural optimization were docked into the binding pocket of AscC and AscC<sup>L180F</sup> using the AutoDockTools-1.5.6. The docking results were then visualized and analyzed by PyMOL.</p></sec><sec id="Sec16"><title>Site-directed mutagenesis of AscC</title><p id="Par30">Alanine scanning was used to identify candidate residues with a Radius within 3 Å, while affinity maturation was employed to design mutants with a focus on those exhibiting a binding energy change exceeding 0.4. Mutants were constructed using overlap extension PCR and then subcloned into the pET28a vector. The primers were listed in Supplementary Table <xref rid="MOESM1" ref-type="media">2</xref>. Each mutant was confirmed by DNA sequencing. Afterward, the resultant variants were transformed into <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> BL21 (DE3) harboring pBbA5c-MevT-MBIS.</p></sec><sec id="Sec17"><title>Qualitative analysis of the products in engineered <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> strains</title><p id="Par31">Calibration curves of compounds <bold>1,</bold> <bold>2</bold>, and <bold>4</bold> were generated using HPLC following the method described above. Five concentration gradients of the isolated compound <bold>1</bold> (80, 40, 20, 10, 5 µg/mL), compound <bold>2</bold> (60, 30, 15, 7.5, 3.75 µg/mL) and compound <bold>4</bold> (500, 250, 125, 62.5, 31.25 µg/mL) were prepared. Each concentration was injected in triplicate, and the linearity of the standard curve was determined by plotting the peak area versus concentration. The resulting equation and correlation coefficient obtained from the linearity study were y = 19252x + 55.984 (R<sup>2</sup> = 0.9981) for compound <bold>1</bold>, y = 13688x + 43.024 (R<sup>2</sup> = 0.9973) for compound <bold>2</bold>, and y = 15153x + 192.93 (R<sup>2</sup> = 0.9993) for compound <bold>4</bold>. The yields of compound <bold>3</bold> were calculated according to the calibration curve of compound <bold>4</bold>.</p></sec><sec id="Sec18"><title>Anti-inflammatory activity assay of compounds 1–4</title><p id="Par32">The assay was performed according to the literature with certain modifications<sup><xref ref-type="bibr" rid="CR43">43</xref></sup>. Briefly, BV-2 microglia cells were cultured in DMEM high-glucose medium containing 10% fetal bovine serum, penicillin (100 U/mL), and streptomycin (100 µg/mL) at 37 °C in a humidified atmosphere with 5% CO<sub>2</sub> and 95% air. BV-2 cells (2×10<sup>5</sup> cells/well) were seeded in 96-well plates and cultured overnight until reaching 70–90% confluence. Subsequently, the cells were pretreated with different concentrations of test compounds (80, 40, 20, 10, and 5 µM) for 1 h, followed by exposure to 1 µg/mL of LPS for 24 h. The levels of TNF-α and IL-6 in the culture supernatants were assessed using enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) kits following the manufacturer’s protocol (BD Biosciences). The absorbance value (A) of each well at 450 nm (570 nm calibration) was measured with a microplate reader (Molecular Devices). Inhibitory rates of TNF-α and IL-6 production were calculated according to the following formula: Inhibitory rate = (1 – (A<sub>sample</sub> – A<sub>blank</sub>)/(A<sub>solvent</sub> – A<sub>blank</sub>)) × 100%. IC<sub>50</sub> values were analyzed by log (inhibitor) vs. normalized response – Variable slope using Prism 9.0 software. Each experiment was conducted with three independent biological replicates.</p></sec><sec id="Sec19"><title>Antimicrobial activity assay of compounds 1–4</title><p id="Par33"><italic toggle="yes">T. rubrum</italic> ATCC4438 was bought from Hospital for Skin Diseases, Institute of Dermatology Chinese of Medical Sciences, Peking Union Medical College. <italic toggle="yes">E. coli</italic> ATCC25922 was bought from HuanKai Microbial Co., Ltd. <italic toggle="yes">S. aureus</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">B. subtilis</italic>, and <italic toggle="yes">M. luteus</italic> were bought from Institute of Resource Insects, Chinese Academy of Forestry Sciences. The antimicrobial assays of compounds <bold>1–4</bold> were conducted using a micro-broth dilution method in 96-well culture plates according to the Clinical and Laboratory Standards Institute (CLSI). Compounds <bold>1–4</bold> were prepared as a 20 mM stock solution in dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO). Ampicillin sodium and cefotaxime sodium were used as the positive agents for the antibacterial assay of Gram-positive bacteria and Gram-negative bacteria, respectively, while terbinafine hydrochloride was used as the positive agent for antifungal activity. The four bacterial strains were cultured on LB agar plates, whereas the fungal strain was cultured on the potato-dextrose agar (PDA) plates. The colonies were taken from the agar plates and incubated in the corresponding medium. The microbe suspension was deliquated with fresh LB or potato-dextrose broth (PDB) to 10<sup>5</sup> cfu/mL. The diluted suspensions were then transferred to a 96-well plate (200 µL per well) containing different concentrations of the test compound. Serial dilutions of the stock solution were performed with final concentrations ranging from 50 to 0.39 µg/mL (including 50, 25, 12.5, 6.25, 3.125, 1.5625, 0.78125, 0.390625 µg/mL). The microplates were incubated at 37 °C for 24 h for the bacteria or at 28 °C for 96 h for the fungi. All assays were performed in quardruplicate. EC<sub>50</sub> was defined as the minimum concentration inhibiting 50% of microbial growth and was calculated using GraphPad Prism 9.0 software.</p></sec><sec id="Sec20"><title>Statistics and reproducibility</title><p id="Par34">We calculated the compound yields synthesized by the engineered strains using peak areas and the calibration curves derived from purified compounds. The compound yields for each strain were measured in triplicate biological replicates, with each replicate originating from different positive clones.</p></sec><sec id="Sec21"><title>Reporting summary</title><p id="Par35">Further information on research design is available in the <xref rid="MOESM4" ref-type="media">Nature Portfolio Reporting Summary</xref> linked to this article.</p></sec></sec><sec id="Sec22" sec-type="supplementary-material"><title>Supplementary information</title><p> <supplementary-material content-type="local-data" id="MOESM1" position="float" orientation="portrait"><media xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_MOESM1_ESM.pdf" position="float" orientation="portrait"><?suppdata-name 42003_2025_7509_MOESM1_ESM.pdf?><?suppdata-size 2560724?><?suppdata-md5 b937e358e4140dc047efed6947c01f00?><?suppdata-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?suppdata-mime-type application?><?suppdata-mime-sub-type pdf?><?suppdata-cloudpmc-urn urn:app:0f1f/11794439/b937e358e414/42003_2025_7509_MOESM1_ESM.pdf?><caption><p>Supplementary Information</p></caption></media></supplementary-material> <supplementary-material content-type="local-data" id="MOESM2" position="float" orientation="portrait"><media xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="42003_2025_7509_MOESM2_ESM.pdf" position="float" orientation="portrait"><?suppdata-name 42003_2025_7509_MOESM2_ESM.pdf?><?suppdata-size 39007?><?suppdata-md5 53a46c814b333bb4734aa0da8c08a20e?><?suppdata-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?suppdata-mime-type application?><?suppdata-mime-sub-type pdf?><?suppdata-cloudpmc-urn urn:app:0f1f/11794439/53a46c814b33/42003_2025_7509_MOESM2_ESM.pdf?><caption><p>Description of Additional Supplementary Files</p></caption></media></supplementary-material>
